Stop Searching Manually, Start Automating Your Job Hunt with AI Tools

If you are still scrolling job boards, filling out forms, and writing every cover letter yourself,
You are wasting hours every week.

The smartest job seekers in 2025 automate it all.

Here is how to use AI tools to save time and land more interviews 👇

1. Find the Right Jobs Automatically
Use tools like Simplify, LazyApply, or Pyjama Jobs to scan hundreds of job boards for you.
→ They filter openings based on your skills, experience, and location and alert you instantly when a perfect match appears.

2. Auto-Fill and Apply in Seconds
Platforms like LazyApply and LoopCV can apply on your behalf.
→ Set your preferences once, and let AI fill applications, send resumes, and track progress automatically.

3. Generate Tailored Resumes and Cover Letters
Use Kickresume, Rezi, or ChatGPT to rewrite your resume and craft role-specific cover letters.
→ Paste the job description, and AI highlights your most relevant achievements in seconds.

4. Automate Follow-Ups and Networking
Use ChatGPT and HoppyCopy to send personalized LinkedIn messages or follow-up emails.
→ Stay top-of-mind with recruiters without sounding robotic.

AI won’t replace your effort, it multiplies it.
Start automating your job search, and focus on what truly matters, acing the interview.

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7384642814690775040

Dear job seekers, if you’re wondering why your LinkedIn isn’t getting recruiter attention - here’s what actually makes a difference (and got me real interview calls):

You don’t need to send 100 connection requests a day.
→ You need a profile that speaks for you - one that clearly shows your impact, skills, and achievements.

You don’t need to copy someone else’s headline.
→ You need a keyword-rich headline that defines who you are and what you do - in just one line. Example: “Product Manager | AI Strategy | Data-Driven Growth.”

You don’t need to write essays in your About section.
→ You need a compelling story - start strong, show measurable results, and let your achievements do the talking.

You don’t need to add every skill you’ve ever learned.
→ You need relevant, updated skills - the ones that match your target roles and show depth, not quantity.

You don’t need to upload fancy designs or videos.
→ You need a clean profile photo with a professional background and a banner that reflects your personal brand.

You don’t need to chase every certification trend.
→ You need to add certifications that align with your goals - AI, data, or management from platforms like Coursera or Udemy.

You don’t need to fill your experience section with job duties.
→ You need to show impact - use metrics like “Increased efficiency by 30%” or “Led a team of 5 engineers.”

You don’t need a thousand recommendations.
→ You need a few strong ones that showcase leadership, teamwork, and tangible results.

You don’t need to wait until graduation to build credibility.
→ You need to showcase projects, articles, or key certifications and make them visible on your profile.

You don’t need to disappear for months.
→ You need to stay active weekly. Comment, share insights, or engage with industry content - visibility builds opportunity.

You don’t need to guess what recruiters search for.
→ You need to research keywords from job descriptions and naturally add them to your headline, summary, and experience.

You don’t need to change jobs to look relevant.
→ You need to keep your profile updated every 3-6 months - your achievements evolve, and so should your LinkedIn.

And remember - recruiters do not look for perfection.
They look for clarity, consistency, and confidence.

So build a profile that reflects who you are, what you’ve achieved, and where you’re headed.

If this post spoke to you and you’re serious about landing your dream role, check out my Job Hunting Accelerator - where we optimize your LinkedIn, resume, and strategy together step by step.

The Harsh Truth: Recruiters Spend Just 7 Seconds on Your Resume.

That’s all it takes for them to decide - shortlist or skip.
In those 7 seconds, your resume either grabs attention or gets ignored.

Here is what they actually look at 👇

Second 1: Your Name & Title
Do they align with the role you applied for?

Second 2: Your Headline / Summary
Does it clearly showcase your key skills and intent?

Second 3: Current Position
Is your latest role relevant and recent enough for this opportunity?

Second 4: Company Names & Timelines
Are there recognizable brands or unexplained gaps?

Second 5: Skills Section
Are the keywords aligned with the job description?

Second 6: Achievements
Do you show measurable results - or just list responsibilities?

Second 7: Decision Time
They decide whether to shortlist or skip. You’ve either won attention - or lost it completely.

Your resume is not just a summary - it is your 7-second sales pitch.
If you want recruiters to stop scrolling and start shortlisting -
you need a resume that’s optimized, quantified, and recruiter-approved.

where Big Tech recruiters help you master AI-powered resume building, job search, and interview prep.

The job market is changing faster than ever and by 2026, the roles that dominate hiring will look very different.

If you want to stay relevant, competitive, and future-proof, you must understand where the industry is heading.

Here is the 2026 Job Market Map, the roles every job seeker should know 👇

1. Software Development Domain
From Front-End, Back-End, Full-Stack, Mobile, QA, to DevOps - these roles power the apps, systems, and products we use daily.

2. Data Domain
Data Analysts, Engineers, BI Developers, DBAs, Data Stewards, ETL Developers - all focused on interpreting, managing, and transforming data for better decisions.

3. Cloud & Infrastructure Domain
Cloud Architects, SREs, Platform Engineers, Network Admins, Cloud Security Engineers, Infrastructure Engineers - the backbone of modern digital systems.

4. Cybersecurity Domain
Cybersecurity Analysts, Pen Testers, SOC Analysts, Security Architects, Incident Response Leads, GRC Specialists - protecting organizations from rising threats.

5. AI & Machine Learning Domain
ML Engineers, AI Researchers, MLOps Engineers, NLP Engineers, GenAI Engineers, Computer Vision Engineers - building the future of intelligent systems.

6. Product & Design Domain
Product Managers, UX/UI Designers, Product Marketers, Product Owners, Design Researchers, Business Analysts - shaping user experience and business outcomes.

Whether you’re switching careers or leveling up, understanding these domains helps you choose the right skills, the right roles, and the right opportunities for 2026 and beyond.

--- Post 96 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025
Engagement: 167 reactions, 27 comments, 7 reposts (Score: 1,119)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7387960479241052161

AI can not attend your interview, but it can help you ace it.

Most candidates prepare randomly, hoping to “wing it.”
Top performers? They follow a clear Interview Preparation Framework that helps them stand out in every round.

Here is what it looks like 👇

1. Research the Company
Understand their business, products, and culture.
→ Read company websites, Glassdoor reviews, and LinkedIn posts.

2. Decode the Job Description
Identify core skills and keywords.
→ Use tools like Jobscan to match your resume perfectly.

3. Prepare Core Answers
Build strong storytelling ability.
→ Practice using the STAR Method (Situation, Task, Action, Result).

4. Know Your Resume Inside Out
Be ready for detailed questions.
→ Review each bullet point with examples and measurable results.

5. Master Common Questions
Gain confidence in standard queries.
→ Prepare for “Tell me about yourself” and “Why us?”.

6. Practice Mock Interviews
Improve tone, clarity, and delivery.
→ Use Pramp, InterviewBuddy, or record yourself.

7. Prepare Smart Questions to Ask
Show curiosity and initiative.
→ Ask about role challenges, growth, and success metrics.

8. Dress & Setup Ready
Look confident and organized.
→ Choose neat attire, clean background, and test your setup.

9. Mindset & Calmness
Control nerves and stay positive.
→ Try 5-min breathing with Calm or Headspace.

10. Post-Interview Actions
Follow up professionally.
→ Send a thank-you email and note key learnings.

If you want to learn how to use AI and proven strategies to land your dream job — That’s exactly what we teach inside the Job Hunting Accelerator, where Big Tech recruiters show you how to win in today’s job market.

Every international student dreams of landing that U.S. job.
But between visa hurdles, rejections, and silence from recruiters -
it can feel impossible.

The truth?
It’s not your talent that’s holding you back.
It’s your strategy.

Here’s what actually works 👇

1. Understand the U.S. Job Market
It’s not about where you studied - it’s about how clearly you communicate results.
Study job descriptions, spot trending skills, and mirror U.S. hiring language.

2. Build a Resume That Speaks Their Language
One page. Clear. Results-driven.
Replace “responsible for” with “improved efficiency by 30%.”
Customize every application.

3. Optimize LinkedIn for Recruiters
Your LinkedIn is your digital handshake.
Use a pro photo, write in first person, and show measurable impact in every role.

4. Network the U.S. Way
Stop asking for jobs - start building relationships.
Connect with alumni, personalize your outreach, and follow up with intent.

5. Communicate With Confidence
Americans value clarity and energy.
Use the STAR method and perfect your 30-second pitch.

6. Apply Strategically, Not Desperately
Don’t apply everywhere.
Target companies that sponsor OPT/H1B and keep a tracker for consistency.

7. Prepare for U.S.-Style Interviews
Know the company. Understand their mission.
Answer behavioral questions with honesty and structure.

8. Use Tools to Simplify the Process
JobScan, Notion, LinkedIn filters - small tools that save big time.
Automation helps you focus on what matters: momentum.

9. Adopt a Growth Mindset
Every rejection is feedback.
Refine, learn, improve and keep moving forward.

10. Find the Right Community
Surround yourself with people who push you higher.
Join groups, attend sessions, and learn from those already doing it.

The truth:
You don’t need luck. You need the right system.

--- Post 101 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025
Engagement: 164 reactions, 25 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 1,096)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7386889582665064448

You’ve applied to 100+ jobs.
Updated your resume.
Refreshed your LinkedIn.
Still - no replies. No interviews. Just silence.
Sounds familiar?
You’re not alone and you’re not unlucky.
You’re just making the same mistakes most job seekers do.

Here’s what’s actually going wrong (and how to fix it) :

- Using the Same Generic Resume
If your resume looks the same for every job, recruiters can tell.
✅ Customize it for each role. Add relevant keywords. Keep it concise.

- Not Researching Companies
Sending mass applications = low conversions.
✅ Learn about company culture, team projects, and tailor your outreach.

- Ignoring ATS Optimization
Your resume might be perfect—but invisible to recruiters.
✅ Use standard titles, avoid graphics, and add role-specific keywords.

- Incomplete LinkedIn Profile
Your LinkedIn is your digital resume.
✅ Add a strong headline, professional photo, and highlight key projects.

- Writing Vague Resume Points
“Responsible for managing tasks” ≠ impact.
✅ Use action verbs, measurable results, and focus on achievements.

- Waiting for Recruiters
No one’s coming to find you - you need to show up.
✅ Send cold emails, message hiring managers, and attend networking events.

- Relying Only on Job Portals
Referrals > applications. Every time.
✅ Leverage alumni networks, LinkedIn outreach, and job communities.

- Sending Generic Connection Requests
“Hi, can we connect?” isn’t networking - it’s noise.
✅ Personalize your messages, mention mutual interests, and follow up politely.

- Giving Up Too Early
Most offers come after the 50th rejection.
✅ Track progress, learn from rejections, and keep improving weekly.

To Crack your dream job You don’t need luck. You need strategy.
Because getting hired today isn’t about applying more, it’s about applying smarter.

============================================================

--- Post 103 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025
Engagement: 163 reactions, 24 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 1,081)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7388985478844334080

Change these 10 settings on LinkedIn and let recruiters find you instead of chasing them.

Most people think they’re not getting interviews because of competition.
But in reality, it’s their LinkedIn settings quietly blocking visibility from recruiters.

Here are 10 simple tweaks that make your profile pop up in more recruiter searches 👇

1. Optimize Your Job Preferences
Recruiters filter candidates based on job preferences first. If yours are vague or outdated, you’ll never appear in their search results. Be specific with roles, locations, and job types like “Data Analyst, Remote, Full-time” instead of just “Analyst.”

2. Turn ON ‘Open to Work’ (Recruiter-Only Mode)
Let recruiters know you’re open to opportunities — without publicly displaying it on your profile. It’s one of the easiest ways to show you’re ready for new roles, privately and professionally.

3. Enable ‘Visibility Outside LinkedIn’
Recruiters don’t just search on LinkedIn, they also use Google. When your profile is visible outside LinkedIn, you show up in more external searches and expand your reach instantly.

4. Manage Connection Requests
If recruiters can’t reach you, they’ll move on. Allowing message requests and InMails ensures hiring managers can contact you directly - no missed DMs, no lost interviews.

5. Update Contact Info
Your contact info is the fastest way for recruiters to reach you. Add a professional email and portfolio link, and make sure they’re visible to your extended network.

6. Turn ON Job Alerts
Being among the first few applicants dramatically boosts your chances. Job alerts help you stay ahead by notifying you the moment relevant roles are posted.

7. Customize Your LinkedIn URL
A clean, personalized URL looks more professional and helps recruiters find you easily. It’s also a great touch for resumes, portfolios, and email signatures.

8. Update Skills & Endorsements
LinkedIn’s search algorithm favors profiles with relevant, up-to-date skills. Refresh your list, add new ones aligned with your industry, and pin the top three that matter most.

9. Use Creator Mode Wisely
Creator Mode boosts visibility for people building an audience but it’s not ideal when you’re actively job hunting. Keep it ON if you’re creating content, OFF if you’re focused on landing interviews.

10. Adjust Profile Viewing Options
Recruiters often revisit profiles they’ve already checked. Staying visible (instead of browsing in private mode) helps them remember you and increases your chances of a callback.

Your LinkedIn profile should work for you, not against you.

Make these 10 small changes today and watch how recruiters start coming to you.

Product Managers - this one’s for you.

We’re entering an era where AI agents are not just assistants… they’re your second brain.

If you’ve ever felt buried under feature requests, feedback loops, or endless meetings - AI agents can help you breathe again.

Here are 10 ways AI Agents can make your job 10x easier :

1. Feature Prioritization Agent — analyzes customer feedback, ranks by impact, and tells you what to build next.

2. Competitor Analysis Agent — monitors your rivals, tracks new launches, and delivers comparison insights.

3. Customer Feedback Agent — gathers reviews, detects pain points, and translates emotions into product insights.

4. Sprint Management Agent — keeps your sprints on track and auto-updates progress for the whole team.

5. Product Roadmap Agent — syncs with Jira or Asana, visualizes milestones, and sends automated updates.

6. Launch Planning Agent — organizes go-to-market checklists and ensures everyone’s aligned pre-launch.

7. User Analytics Agent — tracks adoption, churn, and feature performance in real time.

8. Documentation Assistant Agent — transforms meeting notes into polished PRDs and updates wikis instantly.

9. Risk Assessment Agent — spots delivery blockers and predicts risks before they become problems.

10. Stakeholder Communication Agent — crafts clean updates, highlights wins, and keeps everyone in sync.

AI Agents are not coming to replace Product Managers - they’re coming to elevate them.

The PMs who learn to delegate to AI… will outpace those who don’t.

Author: priteshjagani

Still your resume is getting rejected?

It’s not always your skills that stop you from getting hired - sometimes, it’s how you present them.

Even a great candidate can get ignored if their resume doesn’t pass recruiter screening or ATS filters.

Here are the Top 7 Resume Mistakes that might be holding you back :

1. Using a Generic Resume : Sending the same resume for every job shows no research or customization.
2.  Overloading with Buzzwords : “Team player” and “strategic thinker” mean nothing without real results.
3. Poor Formatting and Design : Cluttered layouts make it hard for both recruiters and ATS to scan.
4. Ignoring Quantifiable Results : Recruiters want numbers, not just responsibilities.
5. Adding Irrelevant Information : Outdated or unrelated details waste precious attention.
6. Neglecting Keywords for ATS : Without the right job-specific keywords, your resume never reaches a human.
7. No Proofreading : Typos and grammar errors instantly break credibility and attention to detail.

Tailor, quantify, and proof every resume. One well-crafted version is worth more than ten generic ones.

--- Post 112 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Oct 16, 2025
Engagement: 182 reactions, 9 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 1,002)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7383124321155715072

Your resume gets you noticed.
Your LinkedIn makes you remembered.

Yet most professionals treat them the same and that’s where they go wrong.
A resume is your formal pitch.
It’s short, crisp, and tailored for a specific job.

Think of it as your movie trailer - quick, powerful, and built to grab attention.
But your LinkedIn?
That’s your digital billboard.
It tells the whole story - your journey, growth, and personality.
It’s where recruiters and clients discover you - even when you’re not applying.

Here’s what recruiters actually look at when comparing your Resume vs LinkedIn :

1. Purpose — Resume applies for jobs; LinkedIn attracts them.
2. Tone & Language — Resume is formal; LinkedIn is conversational and story-driven.
3. Customization — Resume changes per role; LinkedIn stays consistent and broad.
4. Structure & Format — Resume is static; LinkedIn is dynamic and interactive.
5. Depth of Info — Resume highlights results; LinkedIn explains how you achieved them.
6. Validation & Credibility — Resume claims; LinkedIn proves through endorsements.
7. Visibility & Networking — Resume is private; LinkedIn markets you 24/7 globally.

✅ Align both for maximum impact:
Keep titles, skills, and timelines identical.
Use professional tone in your resume and personal touch on LinkedIn.
Let one open doors and the other keep them open.

--- Post 113 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7386124476637192192

Everyone dreams of working in tech abroad.
High salaries. Fancy offices. Global exposure.

But when the paycheck hits your account:
what’s actually left after taxes and rent?

Because a $150K salary doesn’t mean $150K in freedom.
Here’s the real picture :

1. The U.S. tops the chart
$82K left after taxes and living costs — the highest globally.
But also some of the steepest taxes and healthcare costs.

2. Canada follows close
$53K in hand after expenses.
Decent savings, but rising living costs eat into comfort fast.

3. Switzerland stays elite
$51K remaining, high earnings balanced with high-quality living.
Still one of the best work-life-value destinations.

4. New Zealand & Japan - quiet winners
$43K–$49K left over and a more relaxed pace of life.
Less hustle, more harmony.

5. Australia punches above its weight
$41K left with great work-life balance, safety, and sunshine.
One of the most underrated countries for tech pros.

6. Europe’s mixed bag
Germany, Ireland, Belgium - moderate savings.
Spain, Portugal, and France - lifestyle wins, but wallets feel lighter.

The truth:
It’s not about how much you earn, it’s about how much stays with you.

Before choosing your next tech destination,
run the math, not the dream.

Because the best job isn’t just about salary,
it’s about freedom after tax.

--- Post 115 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Jan 14, 2026
Engagement: 191 reactions, 4 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 997)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7413988166308917249

4 pieces of resume advice that have helped international students I know crack internships and jobs at Tesla, Walmart, Capital One, AMD, Cisco, Confluent and more.

1. Pick one role and make your whole resume proof of it

Most international students try to look “flexible”.
Result is a resume that screams “I can do anything” which reads as “I do nothing really well”.

Pick one lane
SDE, Data Analyst, Product Manager, TPM, whatever.

Then rewrite every section to answer one question
“Why is this person a strong fit for THIS role”

That means
- Project titles that match the role language
- Bullets that highlight the skills that role cares about
- Cutting good but irrelevant work

If you are applying to TPM roles, your bullets should show ownership, planning, dependencies, release risk, not just code.

2. Build a master resume from 10 real job descriptions

Do not copy random templates.
Open 10 job descriptions for your target role in your target country.

Highlight what keeps repeating
- Skills
- Tools
- Responsibilities
- Business outcomes

Now build one “master” resume that reflects these patterns

- Use standard headers like Experience, Projects, Skills, Education
- Put the most relevant experience at the top
- Use the same wording the market uses, but only where you actually have evidence

After that, per application, you only adjust a few bullets or keywords, not redesign the whole thing.

3. Write for ATS and busy humans, not for Dribbble

Fancy templates and graphics confuse most ATS systems and annoy recruiters.

Keep it boring and clear
- Simple fonts
- No columns with weird spacing
- No icons, charts, or photo
- PDF with clean text that can be copied

Focus your bullets on outcomes

Instead of
“Worked on Kafka pipelines”

Write
“Built and owned Kafka based data pipeline that reduced report latency from 12 hours to 45 minutes, used by 5 product teams”

Concrete tools + clear result, that is what gets picked in a 6 second skim.

4. Treat your resume like an experiment, not a tattoo

If you have applied seriously for 3 to 4 weeks and your callback rate is near zero, the market is giving you feedback.

Do not just “try harder”.
Change the input.

- Compare your resume with someone from a similar background who is getting calls
- Get one or two honest reviews from people in your target role, not from random friends
- Track your applications in a simple sheet and watch which version of your resume gets more callbacks

The goal is a resume that gets you in the room.

If you are editing fonts more than bullets, your energy is going to the wrong place.

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Jan 14, 2026
Engagement: 185 reactions, 6 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 987)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7412538143419072513

Applying to 1000 jobs in a year and still being unemployed sounds insane and makes you think the market is dead.  "Just apply to more jobs" is terrible advice.

Unless you know which jobs to apply to, when to apply, and how to make yourself impossible to ignore.

Otherwise, you're just spraying resumes into a black hole. let me give you a better strategy based on the results I've seen after coaching 4000+ international students inside my job hunting accelerator.

That meme in this post is funny, but it is exactly what I see every week.
Most people are not job hunting. They are just pressing Easy Apply on autopilot.

Here is the system my best students follow.

1. Fix the way you apply

→ Pick ONE role you are 60 to 80 percent qualified for
→ Create ONE resume that is aligned to that role and to current JDs
→ Make your LinkedIn profile match that same story
→ Configure LinkedIn so recruiters can actually find you
→ Apply to roles within minutes or hours of posting, not weeks later
→ Speed over perfection. First serious applicants usually win
→ Apply to 15 to 25 high fit roles per day
→ Track your numbers like a scientist
→ If 30 plus applications equals 0 interviews, you fix the resume, not the market

2. Turn interviews into offers

→ If 3 plus interviews equals 0 offers, you fix your interview skills
→ Aim to stack multiple interviews in the same week
→ Prepare stories that show ownership, impact and resilience
→ Connect with the interviewer as a person, not just a gatekeeper
→ Stand out with clarity instead of trying to sound like every other candidate
→ Ask questions that make the hiring manager think "I want this person on my team"

3. Treat offers like a system, not a blessing

→ Line up interviews so offers land close together
→ Use competing offers as leverage instead of accepting the first yes
→ Negotiate from a place of data and confidence, not fear
→ Choose the job that fits your life, visa timeline and growth

The market is hard right now.
But for most people, the real problem is not "no jobs".

It is a messy, unplanned hunt with a confused resume and zero strategy.
Fix that first, then watch how different the next 90 days feel.

–

Want to master the Technical Product Manager (TPM) ecosystem?

In 2025, being a TPM is not just about managing backlogs and features. It’s about building a holistic skill set that blends leadership, business acumen, execution excellence, technical fluency, and user empathy to drive products that truly make an impact.

Here’s a breakdown of the TPM Universe you need to master:

1. Leadership & Influence – From driving team alignment and resolving conflicts to coaching teams and making tough decisions under uncertainty, strong leadership sets TPMs apart.

2. Business & Market Acumen – Understanding ROI, pricing strategies, competitive moats, and customer acquisition/retention ensures your product isn’t just built - but thrives in the market.

3. Execution & Delivery – Master agile practices like sprint planning, backlog grooming, and cross-team coordination while managing risks and delivering on time.

🔹 User-Centric Thinking – Great products start with great users. Build empathy through customer journey mapping, usability testing, and accessibility-driven design.

🔹 Data-Driven Decision Making – Leverage analytics tools, A/B testing, and usage insights to validate decisions, measure success, and create feedback loops that continuously improve the product.

🔹 Technical Fluency – A TPM must understand cloud platforms, APIs, system design, and the basics of AI/ML to communicate effectively with engineering teams.

🔹 Product Strategy & Vision – Define clear roadmaps, align with company goals, and balance innovation with market demand to create products that scale.

The TPM of 2025 is not just a manager - they’re a strategic leader at the intersection of business, technology, and users.

Repost to help your network.

--- Post 119 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Dec 15, 2025
Engagement: 173 reactions, 12 comments (Score: 985)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7401651389938204674

14 job hunting lessons that helped this international student (ex-IBM, Google, Microsoft, now Fireworks AI) complete 11 internships and land multiple tech offers:

I recently had Aishwarya Srinivasan on my podcast and what an incredible inspiration she is for all international students out there. 

She moved from India to the U.S. for her master’s at Columbia, broke into AI with no prior U.S. work experience, and went on to land roles at IBM, Google, Microsoft, and now leads at Fireworks AI. Not to mention, a whopping 11 internships on her resume. 

1. During her undergrad in India, Aishwarya did 11 internships because she found the curriculum outdated and wanted hands-on experience. She picked up new languages like R on the job when needed. She didn’t wait for skills, she learned as she went.

2. Instead of waiting for opportunities, she approached startups and offered to work on projects, sometimes as a volunteer. She reached out directly, showing eagerness to contribute, not just asking for jobs. This gave her exposure to real-world tools early.

3. She built her LinkedIn as a portfolio, documenting every project, conference talk, and internship. Her posts were about sharing her work, not trying to go viral or fit an influencer mold. Recruiters and researchers could see her growth at a glance.

4. Networking wasn’t limited to job fairs, she joined university clubs, attended every event, and made a habit of talking to people from other teams, even during coffee breaks. She wanted to hear what others were working on and learn from their experience. This ripple effect led to more invitations and connections.

5. Aishwarya never planned her career path in advance. She made each next move based on what new skills or exposure a role could give her. This openness helped her pivot between IBM, Google, Microsoft, and startups.

6. She didn’t worry about building the perfect resume before starting; instead, she focused on doing the work. Every project or talk became a line on her LinkedIn. Over time, this built a dense, keyword-rich profile that attracted recruiters.

7. When applying for roles, she targeted a handful of companies that matched her interests, not hundreds at random. She focused her learning and networking towards what those specific teams cared about. That’s how her skills aligned closely with hiring needs.

--- Post 120 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Nov 15, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7393331552597946368

A recruiter at Microsoft just told me why international students get rejected before the interview even starts.

I asked her what she wished more F-1 students knew about applying to U.S. companies.

Her answer? Brutal but honest:

"Most international resumes look identical. Same format, same buzzwords, same projects. Nothing tells me who YOU are."

She showed me two piles on her laptop. One had 50+ resumes from international students. The other had 8 she actually wanted to interview.

Here's what separated them:

Your resume screams "template." If it looks like everyone else's, you're invisible. She said Indian students especially use the same resume builders, same action verbs, same layout. "I need to see what makes you different, not just qualified."

You list duties, not impact. "Worked on machine learning model" means nothing. "Built ML model that reduced processing time by 40% for 10K users" gets attention. Numbers and outcomes matter more than titles.

Your experience doesn't translate. She's rejected candidates with strong backgrounds because they didn't explain their work in U.S. context. "If you worked at an Indian startup I've never heard of, help me understand the scale and impact."

You're not showing cultural fit. "International students often focus only on technical skills. But I'm also looking for: Can they communicate in meetings? Will they ask questions? Do they collaborate well?" Soft skills decide who gets the offer.

You seem desperate, not strategic. Applying to 100 jobs with generic resumes fails. She said the students who impressed her had customized applications. "They mentioned our products, referenced our values, showed they actually cared about THIS role."

I've been there. I know how hard it is to stand out when you're competing with hundreds of applicants and don't have a U.S. network yet.

But here's the truth: Your resume isn't just a document. It's your first impression in a country where you're still building credibility.

Make it count. Make it yours.

Being a job seeker in this market is basically a long series of painful events.

It is feeling guilty when you rest.
It is feeling guilty when you grind.
It is surviving more than it is living.
It is losing track of weekends and time.
It is hope that keeps getting postponed.
It is wanting to quit and still showing up.
It is watching your savings quietly disappear.
It is reworking your resume for the 50th time.
It is waking up with hope and sleeping with doubt.
It is getting interview calls and still not feeling safe.
It is smiling on calls while anxiety sits in your chest.
It is preparing hard for interviews that never convert.
It is checking your email before you brush your teeth.
It is forcing confidence while your hands are shaking.
It is being strong for everyone and weak only in private.
It is telling yourself “one more try” when you feel empty.
It is celebrating small wins that no one else understands.
It is pretending to be okay when relatives ask for updates.
It is putting your life on pause for a decision you do not control.
It is questioning your skills on good days and your worth on bad ones.
It is seeing “We regret to inform you” more times than your own name.
It is seeing other people get offers and wondering what you are missing.

Some battles are not meant to be fought loudly.
They are meant to be outlasted.

For everyone still in the waiting phase
Feeling tired does not mean you are weak.
It’s a phase of life, it will pass and you will come out stronger.

I am rooting for you :)

--

If I were an international student in 2025 and wanted to break into tech, I wouldn't waste my time chasing a 4.0 GPA.

I've talked to hundreds of people who work in tech, from mid-level companies to Big Tech.

And here's what I learned: No one cares about your GPA as long as you keep it above 3.0.

Why? Because proof of work beats academic performance every single time.

Let me show you what I mean.

Student A has practical skills with a 3.2 GPA. They've built 3 side projects, contributed to open source, and completed 2 internships.

Student B has a 4.0 GPA but zero proof of work. No projects. No internships. Just perfect grades.

Who gets the job? Student A. Every time.

Companies want to see what you can build, not what you can memorize.
They want proof that you can solve problems, ship code, and work on a team.

Your portfolio matters more than your transcript.
Your ability to explain your projects in an interview matters more than your calculus grade.

So, focus on this instead:
- Network and land opportunities that help you learn practical skills
- Build projects, contribute to open source, join a non-profit/volunteer
- Grab internships, even if they pay less at first, you’ll learn a lot working

Let me conclude the post with this: imagine you were running a team and you had to onboard a developer, who would you choose?

The one who has been building since their 1st year in college, or the one who hasn’t worked on any major project in all of their student life? 

Create proof of work that speaks louder than any GPA ever could.

Secret to Master ChatGPT Prompt Writing !

Prompt writing is not just about asking questions - it’s about guiding ChatGPT with clarity, context, and structure for better results.

This 50-step framework helps you master prompt writing by covering clarity, precision, structure, balance, refinement, and ethics.

1–10: Clarity First
Start with clear purpose, audience, and simple language. Avoid jargon, ambiguity, and ensure grammar and output format are precise.

11–20: Precision & Focus
Use examples, keywords, and clear intent. Avoid overly broad requests, test prompts, and add tone or timeframe when needed.

21–30: Structure & Flow
Don’t assume user knowledge. Keep language neutral, break tasks into steps, add limits, test with inputs, and revise positively.

31–40: Detail & Balance
Ensure logical flow, clarify terms, add background, highlight details, avoid redundancy, use conditions if needed, and balance specificity with flexibility.

41–50: Refinement & Ethics
Highlight steps, use analogies, ensure consistency, verify facts, encourage creativity, refine for clarity, and always prioritize respect and user satisfaction.

Want to write prompts that deliver powerful, accurate results every time? Save this guide and start practicing today! 🔥

Author: priteshjagani

LinkedIn and Indeed have a dirty secret: They don’t remove expired jobs because they want your traffic.

The result? You feel like you got scammed, and they get their ad revenue.

Here’s how to spot a “Zombie Job” before you waste your time.

1. The post is old
If it says “30+ days ago” (or anything beyond 2 weeks), treat it as suspicious. Not always dead, but you need proof it’s alive.

2. The company career page doesn’t show it
Use LinkedIn and Indeed for research. Then go to the company site and search the role.
If the exact position is not listed there, you should move on. 

This one rule alone will save you hours.

3. You keep seeing the same role reposted with zero changes
– Same title.
– Same description.
– Same location.
– Same everything.

4. The role has “500+ applicants” and still looks untouched
High applicant count plus an old posting usually means you’re late to a full inbox. Even if it’s open, you’re fighting a crowd with zero context.

5. The listing is vague on basics
– No hiring manager.
– No team.
– No clear scope.

Good roles tend to be specific because someone actually needs the work done.

A simple 3-step filter you should always use: 

Step 1: Find roles on LinkedIn/Indeed (research phase)
Step 2: Verify on the company careers page (truth phase)
Step 3: If it’s live, apply and then reach out to someone on the team with context

Your time is your most expensive resource in a job search.
Stop feeding it to zombie jobs.

We’ve got modules on everything:
- Networking, resume, job strategy
- focus sessions with the community
- live sessions with Hiring Managers
- guest sessions with recruiters and immigration lawyers

As an international student in the US, you will often feel like you are behind in life. You might be 24, 29, or 32.

Some friends already have H1Bs.
Some are buying cars and houses.
Some are getting promoted while you are still trying to clear your first job search or visa step.

It was simple back home.
Score well in exams, and everyone knows you are doing fine.

Here, it feels like there is a new scoreboard every week.

LinkedIn, Instagram, and WhatsApp groups. Always someone posting a bigger salary, a better company, a faster visa, a nicer apartment.

Slowly, two things happen. You feel smaller, and you start seeing people as competition instead of community.

That is where many people go wrong. 

They stop sharing job links.
They hide how they got referrals.
They gatekeep lawyers, tax tips, and visa updates.
They start resenting the friend who got lucky in the lottery or landed a big offer.

But…

Jealousy will not change your status.
Gatekeeping will not bring you one extra interview.
Bitterness will not pay your rent or build your credit score.

But your actions will always say everything about you.
Their actions will always say everything about them.

So choose your side carefully.

Choose to:

– Share information about visa rules and deadlines when you learn something useful
– Help your friends review their resumes before big applications
– Tell people which tax tools, credit card tips and budgeting tricks are actually working for you
– Celebrate their wins even when your own life feels slow
– Build deep relationships instead of sitting alone with Google and anxiety

Because staying in the US is not just about one job offer or one lottery result.

It is about:

– Knowing your immigration timelines and planning early
– Building a basic personal finance system so you are not one paycheck away from panic
– Having friends who would show up if your visa, health, or job went wrong
– Becoming the kind of person others want to refer to, vouch for, and live with

Kindness is not weakness.
In this country, it is a long-term strategy.

I still hang out with my ex-boss from my Chico days. We play poker, we chat, we hang out, and it’s been over 10 years, one of the best relationships I’ve cultivated in life. 

Think big. 
Don’t let jealousy narrow your vision.

When I was in job-hunting mode, I: 
- Saw 10–15 rejection emails daily
- Messaged 10 people, one would reply, the rest ghosted
- Had screening calls that ended the moment visa sponsorship came up
- Saw batchmates suddenly becoming secretive about their own progress
- Sat refreshing my inbox every hour, hoping for one interview call
- Tied my entire self-worth to outcomes. If I moved forward, I felt capable. If I did not, I felt broken

Everyone said, This is what the market is like.

This is normal.
This is how it works.

One night, I caught myself thinking,
If this is what “chasing a career” feels like, then something is deeply off.

And it was not about the struggle, actually, it was about the way I was carrying it. 

So I changed one small thing.
I stopped letting the outcome decide who I was that day.

I still applied.
I still followed up.
I still prepared just as hard.

But I stopped waking up with dread.
I stopped checking my inbox like it was a verdict on my future.
I stopped allowing silence to define my ability.

Eventually, things moved.
A few calls came in.
Then one offer.
Then another.

And I realized something important.

Job searching is tough. It tests your patience, confidence, and resilience.
But it does not have to erase your sense of self.

And it absolutely does not get to decide your worth as a person.

The market will improve.
The phase will pass.

Make sure you still recognise yourself when it does.

Marrying a green card holder almost ruined her American dream. Let me tell you the story of Priya, who was on her OPT visa. When she got married, like most people, she assumed filing her marriage paperwork meant she could just stay, work, and travel peacefully.

That's exactly where she was wrong. US immigration law treats marrying a green card holder and a US citizen completely differently.

If you marry a US citizen, you're considered an immediate relative. No visa caps. No waitlist. You can file everything at once and get your work permit and travel permit in a few months.

Priya married a green card holder.

She comes under the F2A category. That category has a waitlist that can easily cross 24 months.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. This question came up, and the answer surprised many people. 

Also, if you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

The scariest part: During that wait, her OPT expired. Suddenly, she couldn't work legally. If she stepped out of the US, she couldn't come back.

She was stuck waiting for F2A processing with no work authorization and no ability to travel.

➤ The One Thing That Saved Her:

While she was waiting, her husband became eligible to apply for US citizenship. The moment he naturalized, her case was instantly upgraded.

She skipped the line because spouses of US citizens are immediate relatives with no waiting period.

Before you assume marriage automatically fixes your visa status, understand exactly which category you fall under.

Marrying a US citizen = Immediate relative, fast processing
Marrying a green card holder = F2A category, 24+ month wait

If your OPT or work authorization is expiring soon and you're planning to get married, talk to an immigration attorney about timing and which category you'll be in.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only. If you're considering marriage-based adjustment of status, speak with a qualified immigration attorney about your specific situation.

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Mar 15, 2026

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7437176219412058112

If I had only 90 days to find my next role and had to make a job search strategy that would help me without drowning in 1000+ job applications, I would do exactly this:

1. Build your “People Map” before you apply anywhere

Open a sheet and list 60 to 100 people you already know: former coworkers, seniors, alumni, friends, meetup contacts, professors, classmates, roommates, anyone.

Add 3 columns:
➸ Name
➸ Company
➸ Who else they can connect you to

This becomes your personal job portal.

Btw, I totally understand that job-hunting in 2026 is extremely tiring, long, and painful. 

That's why if this market is draining you and you want structured help on resume, outreach, and strategy, you can explore my Job Hunting Accelerator: 5,100+ people are already using it to land offers. 

Link: https://lnkd.in/gt5YBAbr

2. Stop asking “Are you hiring?”

That question gets ignored. Instead, send this:

“Hey [Name], I’m actively looking for [Role]. If you see anything relevant at [Company] or in your network, I’d love a quick pointer. I can share a 2 line summary of what I do best.”

Low pressure. 
Easy to respond to. 
No desperate energy.

3. Apply less, but apply with intent

Every day:
➸ Apply to 20 to 30 roles max
➸ Shortlist the 10 roles that are truly a fit
➸ For those 10, do outreach the same day

The only thing worse than applying to 1000 jobs is applying to 1000 jobs with zero follow-up.

4. Email the hiring manager even if you have no referral

Referrals help, but they’re not the whole game.
Your advantage is speed + clarity.

Use a simple 3 line email:
Line 1: Who you are + your most relevant proof
Line 2: Why you’re a fit for this exact role
Line 3: Ask for a quick screen and attach resume

Keep it brief. No essays.

5. Use LinkedIn for “hidden roles,” not Easy Apply

Search your title and filter by “Posts.” You’ll find hiring managers and recruiters posting roles that never even make it to job boards. Those are the easiest doors to open.

6. Make interview prep a daily ritual, not a weekend plan

If you’re on a clock, you don’t “start preparing” after you get interviews.

Every day:
➸ 60 minutes role-specific prep
➸ 30 minutes mock or question drilling
➸ One story rewrite (your STAR examples)

Your stories are what gets you hired.

7. Use AI like a teammate

Use it to:
➸ Rewrite your outreach in your tone
➸ Drill your resume like an interviewer
➸ Generate role-specific questions from your resume + JD

But you still show up with the thinking.

8. Track everything like a pipeline

Conversations are the metric here. 

Track:
➸ People contacted
➸ Replies
➸ Screens booked
➸ Follow-ups sent
➸ Interviews in progress

If replies are low, fix messaging.
If screens are low, fix positioning.
If final rounds fail, fix interview execution.

Don’t take a student loan in India to study abroad without knowing this. This one decision can save you thousands of dollars over the next 10 to 15 years.

12 years ago, when I did a rough calculation of how much it would cost to study in the U.S.A., only one word came to mind: Impossible.

When I went from bank to bank, it only made that feeling worse.

I had
- No collateral
- No jewellery or property to show
- No cosigner who could sign the loan for me

On paper, I was just another kid with a dream and zero assets. So most banks treated me like that. Fast forward to today, and things have changed for the better.

I have worked with MPOWER Financing for 4+ years and done multiple workshops with them. I have seen students who would have given up on their admission actually make it to the U.S. because of how MPOWER works.

If you are considering education loans, here is why I consider them among the best options for the U.S. and Canada.

1. Fixed interest rate from day one. The rate you sign up for is the rate you finish with.
2. No collateral, no cosigner, no proof of income. They look at your admit, your potential, your program and your future earning capacity. This is a game-changer for students who do not want to put family property or parents’ retirement on the line.
3. Free sanction letter you can use for visa, and I-20. Once your loan is approved, you get a sanction letter that works as proof of funds. You can use it for your I-20 and visa interview.
4. Origination fee built into the loan. There is a 5 per cent origination fee, but you do not pay it from your pocket upfront. It is deducted from the loan amount after disbursement.
5. You start building your U.S. credit score. The loan is in U.S. dollars, in your name, with a U.S. lender. Once you land and start making the small interest payments after classes begin, you are already building a credit history.

Over the last decade, MPOWER has sanctioned loans for more than 1,50,000 students.

If you are planning to study in the U.S. or Canada, do not blindly sign any loan, including MPOWER. Compare everything.

- Read the fine print.
- Understand fixed vs floating.
- Think about your future income in dollars, not just interest rates in rupees.

It is definitely not easy to be a job seeker and an international student in the USA.

To make it, you are expected to handle things most people never even think about:

1. Spend 30 to 60 Lakhs on education with zero guarantee of a job at the end.
2. Work twice as hard for half the certainty.
3. Compete with students who grew up here, know the culture, and already have networks.
4. Apply to hundreds of roles where your resume is filtered out because of your visa status.
5. Learn new technologies at a pace you have never experienced before just to stay relevant.
6. Build U.S. experience while being rejected for not having U.S. experience.
7. Balance coursework, projects, rent, groceries, CPT rules, and part-time shifts.
8. Prepare for interviews with the pressure of OPT and STEM OPT deadlines behind you.
9. Live far from home, often alone, while pretending everything is fine on video calls.
10. Watch friends get offers before you and still keep your confidence steady.
11. Manage constant doubt about finances, career, identity, and the future.
12. Know that one bad term, one missed opportunity, or one market shift can reset everything.

Being an international student is not just hard.
It is consistently demanding in a way that never fully turns off.

Over the years, I’ve met hundreds of students who went through this journey.

Some made it.
Some also returned home.

This path itself is unforgiving.

Yet, despite everything, thousands choose this path every year.

Because the opportunities can change not just your life, but your entire family’s future.

Because one offer here can lift years of struggle back home.

While this journey is difficult, it opens doors you cannot find anywhere else.

As long as you know and understand the reality, it’s just a matter of if and not when things work out for you. Talent, skill, and capability are on hand, but mental strength and the right mindset are even more important.

Applying to 1000 jobs in a year and still being unemployed sounds insane and makes you think the market is dead.  "Just apply to more jobs" is terrible advice.

Unless you know which jobs to apply to, when to apply, and how to make yourself impossible to ignore.

Otherwise, you're just spraying resumes into a black hole. Let me give you a better strategy based on the results I've seen. 

Btw, if you're finding your job hunt journey extremely tiring, long, and stuck in place forever with 100s of rejections and want help.. with your resume, outreach, or strategy. 

You can explore my Job Hunting Accelerator: 5,100+ people are already using it to land offers. 

Link: https://lnkd.in/gt5YBAbr

Here is the system my best students follow.

1. Fix the way you apply

→ Pick ONE role you are 60 to 80 percent qualified for
→ Create ONE resume that is aligned to that role and to current JDs
→ Make your LinkedIn profile match that same story
→ Configure LinkedIn so recruiters can actually find you
→ Apply to roles within minutes or hours of posting, not weeks later
→ Speed over perfection. First serious applicants usually win
→ Apply to 15 to 25 high fit roles per day
→ Track your numbers like a scientist
→ If 30 plus applications equals 0 interviews, you fix the resume, not the market

2. Turn interviews into offers

→ If 3 plus interviews equals 0 offers, you fix your interview skills
→ Aim to stack multiple interviews in the same week
→ Prepare stories that show ownership, impact and resilience
→ Connect with the interviewer as a person, not just a gatekeeper
→ Stand out with clarity instead of trying to sound like every other candidate
→ Ask questions that make the hiring manager think "I want this person on my team"

3. Treat offers like a system, not a blessing

→ Line up interviews so offers land close together
→ Use competing offers as leverage instead of accepting the first yes
→ Negotiate from a place of data and confidence, not fear
→ Choose the job that fits your life, visa timeline and growth

The market is hard right now.
But for most people, the real problem is not "no jobs".

It is a messy, unplanned hunt with a confused resume and zero strategy.
Fix that first, then watch how different the next 90 days feel.

============================================================

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Oct 16, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7383924637358825472

Attention job seekers, if you’re struggling to connect with hiring managers or industry leaders right now, here’s what actually works (and got me real responses from top professionals):

You don’t need to spam 100 connection requests a day.
→ You need to engage before you message. Leave thoughtful comments on their posts - it warms up the connection before you ever hit “Connect.”

You don’t need to send generic “Let’s connect” messages.
→ You need to personalize every message. Mention something specific they’ve done or shared. People reply to genuine interest, not templates.

You don’t need to ask for referrals right away.
→ You need to add value first. Share an article, insight, or perspective that aligns with what they care about.

You don’t need to message only when you need something.
→ You need to start conversations that matter. Ask thoughtful, open-ended questions instead of transactional requests.

You don’t need to feel awkward reaching out cold.
→ You need to leverage mutual connections. A warm introduction builds trust faster than any random DM ever could.

You don’t need to network once and disappear.
→ You need to stay consistent. Engage with their content weekly - comment, react, or share genuine thoughts.

You don’t need to attend every random event.
→ You need to join communities where your peers and mentors hang out - Slack groups, forums, or professional meetups that actually lead to relationships.

You don’t need to wait until you have “something big” to share.
→ You need to share your learnings and insights regularly. People connect with authenticity, not perfection.

You don’t need to chase every recruiter.
→ You need to target smartly. Use LinkedIn filters and alumni networks to reach the right hiring managers for your role.

And you definitely don’t need to vanish after one message.
→ You need to follow up professionally. A simple, polite message a week later keeps the conversation alive.

Networking is not about chasing - it’s about creating genuine relationships that lead to opportunities.

So remember this:
Don’t overthink connection.
Start small. Engage smart. Stay consistent.
And momentum will follow.

If this spoke to you and you want to learn how to build authentic industry connections that actually lead to interviews and offers, check out my Career Networking Accelerator - where we fix your LinkedIn, outreach strategy, and engagement plan together step by step.

Ever wondered how world-class products are built, from idea to launch?

The secret lies in mastering the Product Management Lifecycle.

Most people think product management is just about launching features…

But in reality, it’s a journey - from discovery to continuous improvement.
Each stage has its own purpose, challenges, and deliverables.
Miss one layer, and your entire product foundation weakens.

Here are the 8 Stages of the Product Management Lifecycle every PM should know :

1. Research & Discovery Layer — Understand users, define problems, and analyze trends before building anything.
2. Ideation & Strategy Layer — Turn insights into clear strategies, goals, and roadmaps that guide your product’s direction.
3. Planning & Requirements Layer — Create PRDs, map user stories, and evaluate feasibility to align the team’s vision.
4. Design & Prototyping Layer — Build wireframes, test prototypes, and refine the user experience through feedback.
5. Development & Execution Layer — Implement features, integrate APIs, and run sprints to bring your product to life.
6. Testing & Quality Assurance Layer — Test, validate, and refine through performance and user feedback loops.
7. Launch & Go-to-Market Layer — Prepare your marketing, sales, and product teams for a seamless rollout.
8. Monitoring & Continuous Improvement Layer — Analyze usage data, gather feedback, and iterate for long-term success.

Great product are built not by luck, but with layered precision

Master each stage, and you’ll build products that don’t just launch but will lead.

Your LinkedIn profile sucks.

That’s the brutal truth for many job seekers out there.

You could be overqualified for your dream role, yet lose it to someone who simply shows up better online.

Because in today’s world, perception is power.

Your profile is not just a resume - it’s your personal brand.
And if it doesn’t speak for you, recruiters won’t either.

The good news? You can fix it in a few simple steps.

Here are the 7 LinkedIn mistakes silently killing your chances of getting hired :

1. Incomplete Profile — Missing photo, headline, or details make you look inactive.
2. Generic Headline — “Hardworking individual” isn’t memorable. Be specific and keyword-rich.
3. Empty About Section — Without your story, recruiters can’t connect with you personally.
4. No Content or Branding — A silent profile is an invisible one. Stay active.
5. No Recommendations — No social proof means no trust. Request and give endorsements.
6. No Custom URL — A messy link looks careless. Customize it for a professional look.
7. Unaligned Resume — If your resume and LinkedIn don’t match, you lose credibility.

Your LinkedIn is your digital handshake - make it firm and confident.

Author: priteshjagani

Girish Runnesh Jagadeesan came to the US in Fall 2024 for his MS in Robotics at Purdue University Autonomy, Internet of Things (IoT), and Robotics Professional Master's Programs, in what everyone was calling the worst job market for international students. Within six months of landing, he already had three internship offers in robotics with just 1 year of experience in India. 

A big part of that was because Girish did that work before he applied.

He was very specific: he wanted to work in control systems and automation. That decision shaped everything that followed.

Before Purdue, he had:

- A bachelor’s in Robotics and Automation with a focus on control systems
- One year as a development engineer, where he worked directly on industrial control problems

Instead of dumping every tool he had used, he rebuilt his resume around outcomes. He:
-  Quantified almost every bullet
-  Built a portfolio site that showed projects
-  Practised explaining projects so his interviews sounded structured

Then came outreach. With a few close friends, he:
- Shortlisted companies in robotics, automation, and logistics
- Looked up people on LinkedIn who worked on control systems
- Sent short, specific messages that mentioned a project or a problem

He started applying in October.  His clear advice is to start applications, resume work, and outreach before landing in the US.

The results, he landed 3 roles:
- Junior Robotics Engineer at RoboChef
- Systems Engineer at Dexterity
- Offer from Starship Technologies

Purdue’s Robotics program played a big role here as well:

- Flexible coursework so he could go deep into control systems and automation
- Projects designed with industry involvement, which felt like "free internships"
- A brand name that already had trust at companies like Dexterity, where many hires come from Stanford, CMU, NYU and similar schools

What you can copy from Girish and Purdue Robotics:
→ Get at least some work experience before a master’s if you can, even one year
→ Decide your niche before you apply, not after you arrive
→ Prepare resume, LinkedIn, and a small portfolio before landing in the US
→ Quantify your work and cut bullets that only list tools
→ Customise your resume for every role instead of sending one version everywhere
→ Treat networking and referrals like a weekly task, not a last minute hack
→ Choose programs where projects and co ops are tightly connected to industry

To hear Girish’s full experience in his own words:
Watch the full podcast →https://lnkd.in/gdkVq8hx

If you are exploring Robotics or Control Systems in the US as an international student:
Take a closer look at what Purdue’s Robotics program offers → https://lnkd.in/gRpE3m2A

Are You Ready for a Tech Interview? Think Again.

Top tech companies don’t just test your coding skills - they test how you think, communicate, and adapt under pressure.

That’s why interviews often hit you with tough questions designed to reveal your true potential. If you’re not prepared, these can derail your chances.

Here are 9 tough questions you’ll almost certainly face (and what they really mean :

1. Walk Me Through Your Background → They want a clear story: Present → Past → Future.
2. Biggest Achievement → Show results, ownership, and measurable impact.
3. Why This Role, Why Now? → Prove your motivation aligns with company goals.
4. A Project That Failed → They look for honesty, resilience, and lessons learned.
5. Handling Conflicts → Emotional intelligence and teamwork under pressure.
6. Ideal Work Environment → Fit with culture and adaptability.
7. Vision and 3-Year Plan → Ambition aligned with company direction.
8. Questions for Us? → Proof you’re evaluating them seriously.
9. Why Should We Hire You? → Your confident value pitch that solves their problems.

Pro Tip: Most candidates stumble because they answer vaguely. Instead, use real stories and measurable outcomes to stand out.

Save this and practice before your next big interview.

International students often think the solution to job hunting is simple:

Apply more.

But the real problem is conversion rate.

Cold applications today convert at roughly 0.5%–1%.

Referrals convert at 10%–20%.

That’s almost 10x higher.

So instead of sending 500 random applications, try the 50 Job Strategy:

1️⃣ Identify H-1B friendly companies
2️⃣ Find 2 employees inside the company for each role
3️⃣ Build a connection before applying
4️⃣ When you apply, someone internally can vouch for you

Because hiring managers trust people more than resumes.

50 smart applications > 500 cold applications.

Project management isn't what they teach you.

Real PM work is 80% psychology, 20% spreadsheets.

I learned this the hard way working with international students navigating their U.S. journey.

They come asking: "Which framework should I use?"

Wrong question.

The right question: "Which framework fits this situation?"

• Waterfall: When requirements are crystal clear and won't change (visa applications, compliance projects)

• Agile: When you're building something new and need rapid feedback (job search strategy, portfolio development)

• Scrum: When your team needs structure but flexibility (group projects with tight deadlines)

• Kanban: When work flows continuously and priorities shift daily (networking outreach, application tracking)

• Lean: When you need to eliminate waste and focus on value (interview prep, skill development)

Don't pick just one framework and force every project into it.

That's like using a hammer for everything.

The best PMs I know switch frameworks based on context.

Not personal preference.

Not what's trendy.

Context.

Which frameworks have you used and for what kind of projects?

P.S. Follow Pritesh Jagani for content like project managent and getting a job in tech field. 
Repost to help your network.

The March 2026 Visa Bulletin just dropped. This is one of the biggest forward movements we’ve seen in years in the USA.

If you’re in EB1 or EB2, especially from India or the rest of the world, this could be your moment. I’ll explain in simple terms.

What is the Visa Bulletin and “priority date”?
Think of the green card process like a long queue.

➤  Your priority date = the day you officially entered the queue
(usually the PERM filing date or I-140 filing date).

➤ Every month, the Visa Bulletin says:
“We are now serving people who got in line on or before this date.”

Btw, every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with lawyers from Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. We talk about the news like this every week, so make sure you tune in weekly. 

Also, If you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

If the date in the bulletin is after your priority date, you are “current”.
If it is before your priority date, you are still waiting.

The bulletin has two charts:
- Filing Dates (Dates for Filing) – who can submit I-485.
- Final Action Dates – for whose I-485 can be approved.

1) EB1 movement

EB1 is for priority workers – managers, researchers, people with very strong profiles.

Filing Dates
➤ India and China jumped forward about 4 months.
➤ Mexico, Philippines, Rest of World stay current.

Final Action Dates
➤ India and China moved forward about 1 month.
➤ Mexico, Philippines, Rest of World stay current.

Translation: EB1 India and China are still backlogged, but they are moving, not frozen.

2) EB2 movement

EB2 is for advanced degree professionals and people with exceptional ability. This is where the big action is.

Filing Dates
➤ India jumped forward around 11 months.
➤ China did not move.
➤ Mexico, Philippines, Rest of World became current after a large jump.

Final Action Dates
➤ India moved forward about 2 months.
➤ China stayed the same.
➤ Mexico, Philippines, Rest of World moved forward roughly 6 months to 15 Oct 2024.

Translation:
Many EB2 applicants outside India and China are now at the front of the line. EB2 India is still backlogged, but the dates are clearly moving forward.

3) Why this bulletin matters?

We still have one open question: USCIS will announce whether March filings can use Filing Dates or must use Final Action Dates. That update decides how many people can actually submit I-485 in March.

Even without that decision, the signal is clear: EB1 is moving slowly but steadily.
EB2 just had an unusually strong forward jump, especially for India Filing Dates and for the rest of world.

Btw, if you're finding your job hunt journey extremely tiring, long, and stuck in place forever with 100s of rejections and want help.. with your resume, outreach, or strategy. 

You can explore my Job Hunting Accelerator: 5,100+ people are already using it to land offers.

1. The post is old

If it says “30+ days ago” (or anything beyond 2 weeks), treat it as suspicious. Not always dead, but you need proof it’s alive.

2. The company career page doesn’t show it

Use LinkedIn and Indeed for research. Then go to the company site and search the role. If the exact position is not listed there, you should move on. 

This one rule alone will save you hours.

3. You keep seeing the same role reposted with zero changes
– Same title.
– Same description.
– Same location.
– Same everything.

4. The role has “500+ applicants” and still looks untouched
High applicant count plus an old posting usually means you’re late to a full inbox. Even if it’s open, you’re fighting a crowd with zero context.

5. The listing is vague on basics
– No hiring manager.
– No team.
– No clear scope.

Good roles tend to be specific because someone actually needs the work done.

A simple 3-step filter you should always use: 

Step 1: Find roles on LinkedIn/Indeed (research phase)
Step 2: Verify on the company careers page (truth phase)
Step 3: If it’s live, apply and then reach out to someone on the team with context

Your time is your most expensive resource in a job search.
Stop feeding it to zombie jobs.

"My H-1B is approved, but I'm in India, and there are no stamping appointment slots available. When will appointments open, and how long is the wait?"

This is one of the most stressful situations H-1B holders are dealing with right now.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with a lawyer from Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. Last week, this exact question came up, and the strategy shared is something most people don't know about.

Btw, if you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, or other talent-based visas, grab a free 30-min consultation to see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

[1] You can request an emergency appointment based on urgent need, but you have to provide solid evidence that you physically need to be in the US. And there's a catch: you need a regular appointment scheduled first before you can even request one.

[2] How to catch those slots?  Stay up late. Check at 2 AM, 3 AM, 4 AM India time. Canceled slots open throughout the night as US-based cancellations roll in, but you have to be awake and refreshing the page to catch them.

Grab any slot, even if it's months away. Once you have a regular appointment on the books, you can use it to request an emergency date based on urgent circumstances.

A lot of H-1B holders who had appointments booked are now canceling trips, given the current situation. Those slots go back into the pool at random times, usually in the middle of the night India time.

–
TL;DR: 

1. Stay up late (2–4 AM India time) and refresh the appointment booking page
2. Grab any available slot, even if it's far out
3. Use that appointment to request an emergency one if needed
4. Google "manifest visa emergency appointment guide" for the exact process

Disclaimer: I'm not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only, based on conversations with immigration lawyers. Consider consulting an immigration attorney about emergency appointment procedures.

The “Easy Apply” button on LinkedIn is a graveyard of applications.
And sending a generic “just checking in” DM to a recruiter is just digging the hole deeper.

Most people do something
Very few do the right thing

For a single role:
↣1,000+ people click Apply
↣ ~10% send a LinkedIn DM
↣ <1% send a proper follow-up email
↣ ~0.01% follow up twice and do it well

Those 0.01% are the ones recruiters remember. Here’s a simple framework to network with hiring teams without sounding needy, while still making your case.

Step 1: Apply first. Always.

Never reach out without applying.

Your message should reference a specific role and application.

Generic:
“Hi, I’m exploring opportunities.”

Good:
“I applied for the Supply Chain Intern role last week and wanted to briefly follow up.”

Step 2: Reach the right person

Don’t message random engineers.
Don’t message generic HR inboxes.

Find one of these:
↣ Recruiter tied to the role
↣ Hiring manager
↣ Team member from the same function

Step 3: Your first message should do only ONE thing

Your goal is not to sell yourself.
Your goal is to earn a reply.

Structure:
↣ One line of context
↣ One line of relevance
↣ One clear ask

Example:

“Hi [Name], I met your team at the career fair and applied for the Supply Chain Intern role. I have prior experience working on demand planning tools used in your org. Would you be open to forwarding my profile to the hiring team if relevant?”

Short. Calm. Specific.

Step 4: Follow up more than once

This is where most people quit too early.

One message is invisible.
Two messages is polite.
Three to five messages is persistence.

Follow up every 7 to 10 days.
Same thread. Same tone.

Step 5: Let consistency do the work

The people who win:
• Start applying months early
• Get comfortable with rejection
• Iterate resumes quietly
• Keep following up without emotion

They trust the process, not the dopamine of instant replies.

Job hunting is not about being aggressive.
It’s about being visible without being annoying.

The difference between silence and an interview is often just one well-timed follow-up.

If you’re job hunting right now, especially on a visa clock, stop doing more pray and spray applications.

Start doing better follow-ups.

Save this.
You’ll need it.

—

Product Manager vs Project Manager vs Technical Program Manager : What's the difference between ?

They sound similar but their roles, focus, and success metrics are very different.

Here’s the Breakdown

- A Product Manager (PM) defines what should be built and why. They own the product vision, strategy, and roadmap, bridging customer needs with business goals. Success for a PM is measured by product adoption, impact, and customer satisfaction. Example: A Spotify PM defining playlist personalization features.

- A Project Manager (PjM) ensures projects are delivered on time and within scope. They manage timelines, budgets, and cross-functional teams while tracking risks and dependencies. Their success is tied to stakeholder satisfaction and on-time delivery. Example: A consulting PjM rolling out a client’s CRM system.

- A Technical Program Manager (TPM) manages large-scale, cross-functional technical initiatives. They translate high-level goals into execution, remove roadblocks, and align engineering efforts across teams. Success here is measured by the seamless execution of complex technical programs. Example: A Google TPM coordinating the rollout of a cloud infrastructure program.

Key Differences at a Glance
- Product Manager → Focuses on product vision, features, and user adoption.
- Project Manager → Focuses on scope, timelines, and delivery.
- Technical Program Manager → Focuses on large-scale technical execution and cross-team alignment.

Which role do you see yourself growing into - PM, PjM, or TPM?

P.S. Follow Pritesh Jagani for content like project managent and getting a job in tech field. 

Repost to help your network.

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Mar 15, 2026
Engagement: 120 reactions, 3 comments, 3 reposts (Score: 636)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7432841872663908354

I’ve talked to 100s of international students in the last 6 years while travelling to 50+ Universities in the USA. I’ve met people who landed offers worth $100k+ even with 0 relevant experience in the domain.

Here is how you can create one too:

1. Tailor every single application

Most students send one resume to 50 roles and then wonder why nothing moves.
The ones who land real offers treat every application like a mini project.

- They read the JD properly
- Pick 2 to 3 skills that are repeated
- Rewrite bullets to show those skills in action

Examples

- For a PM role, they highlight collaboration, ownership, leadership
- For a data role, they push SQL, dashboards, experiments, insights
- For a backend role, they bring up APIs, scale, reliability

Takeaway

- Highlight 2 to 3 key skills from the JD
- Rewrite bullets under your experience to point to those skills
- Remove side jobs or projects that do not support the story

Template
"Aligned [project or experience] with [specific role] by focusing on [key skill or impact]."

2. Metrics speak louder than words

A good resume does not just say what you did. It shows what changed because you did it.

Examples

- "Led a team of 4 to design a prototype that reduced manual data entry by 20 percent."
- "Analyzed 5 years of data and found patterns that improved process efficiency by 15 percent."
- "Automated a report that saved 10 hours per week for the team."

Takeaway

- Start with an action verb: designed, implemented, led, automated
- Add a clear result: increased efficiency, reduced time, improved accuracy
- End with a number: percent, hours saved, money saved, users impacted

Template
"[Action verb] [task or initiative] that resulted in [specific impact] by [metric]."

3. The resume structure matters

You do not need a fancy template. You need something that is easy to scan.

Key tips

- Put experience above education, even as a student, if you have internships or solid projects
- Keep a clean skills section where tools like SQL, Python, Tableau, Figma, Jira are easy to find
- Use bullets instead of paragraphs and start every line with an action verb

A recruiter should understand who you are and what you bring in under 10 seconds.
Your structure decides whether they get that chance or not.

4. Start early, iterate often

Strong resumes are not written the night before applications open.

The people who do well

- Draft their resume months before they start applying
- Ask seniors, alumni, and mentors to review it
- Update it as they finish each project or internship

They treat the resume like a living document, not a one-time assignment.

Takeaway

- Create a first draft early
- Get feedback from people who already have the roles you want
- Keep refining it based on actual JDs and interview results

What separates a good Product Manager from a truly top-performing one?

It’s not just about shipping features, it’s about mastering clarity, empathy, and resilience while aligning business goals with customer needs.

A high-performing PM knows how to prioritize what matters most, simplify complex problems into actionable steps, and communicate clearly across diverse teams. They balance strategy with execution, speak up for the long-term vision, and stay calm under pressure.

But just as important - great PMs show empathy for both users and teams, embrace failures as learning opportunities, and continuously sharpen their self-awareness. These habits don’t just deliver products - they build trust, alignment, and long-term impact.

Whether you’re starting your PM journey or already leading products, these principles can help you step up and thrive in one of the most challenging roles in tech.

Save this framework, share it with your team, and ask yourself: Which of these areas do I need to level up in right now?
