In 2014, I took the biggest emotional and financial bet of my life.
I was over $20,000 in debt, alone on this journey, with no roadmap.

That amount felt huge for me at that time. It could have felt like a burden, but I chose to see it as my responsibility. I had said yes to this dream, so I decided to show up for it fully and make my Master’s count.

During my Master’s, I made a few quiet promises to myself:

– If I am paying in dollars, I will treat every class, project, and conversation like an investment
– If I feel lost, I will ask for help, talk to seniors, professors, and professionals instead of staying stuck alone.
– If things get hard, I will still keep taking the next small step, even if I cannot see the entire path.

I was not waiting for a perfect plan. I was building my own.
My job hunting journey was no less challenging.

When I was in job-hunting mode, I:
– Saw 10–15 rejection emails daily
–  Messaged 10 people, one would reply, the rest stayed silent
–  Had screening calls that ended the moment visa sponsorship came up
–  Saw batchmates suddenly becoming secretive about their own progress
– Sat refreshing my inbox every hour, hoping for one interview call

So I treated all of this as information, not a verdict.

I dug in, figured out my gaps, and spoke to mentors who had already done what I wanted to do. Step by step, I refined my approach, my resume, and the way I spoke about my work.

By the end of 2016, I had 3 offers on the table, and I could choose the one that fit me best.

I spent over 6 years at my first job at EXL Health, with multiple promotions and a lot of learning. I grew from “just grateful to be here” to someone who could lead, take ownership, and add value.

Now, years later, I balance a demanding full time role with a creator life. Thousands of students from different parts of the world learn with us. I talk about careers, visas, fear, confidence, and purpose, because I have lived every part of that journey from confusion to clarity.

If you are in the middle of your own bet right now, here is what I want you to remember:

You chose this path.
You are capable of learning what you do not yet know.
You can turn debt, pressure, and uncertainty into direction, skills, and progress.

Own your choice.
Do the work with intention.
Your ability to create a different future is far bigger than your current situation.

Things I would tell you as your Hiring Manager and Your Job Hunt Coach (Who's running a community of 4500+ Jobseekers) if I wasn’t afraid of hurting your feelings:

First, let us fix something basic.

[1] Please remove your photograph from your resume.

I am sure it is a great picture. It belongs on your LinkedIn profile, not on a document that is meant to be read by software before a human ever sees it. 

An ATS cannot judge confidence, posture, or professionalism. It only reads text. Anything visual only increases the chances of your resume breaking during parsing.

[2] Second, your resume does not need to look impressive. It needs to be readable.

Over-designed resumes feel premium to you, but they quietly lose you interviews. Columns, icons, graphics, progress bars, logos, fancy fonts. All of this interferes with how applicant tracking systems extract your data. 

I have seen strong candidates lose entire experience sections simply because the formatting could not be parsed properly. A simple layout converts better than a beautiful one. Every single time.

[3] Third, if a recruiter offers you a prep call and you skip it, you are playing ego games with your own career.

That 15 minute call is not a formality. It is the recruiter telling you what the hiring manager actually cares about. 

It is the one place where you can ask, What should I avoid. What do they value most. What will make me stand out. When a recruiter helps you prepare, both of you win if you succeed. Use that leverage.

- focus sessions with the community
- live sessions with Hiring Managers
- guest sessions with recruiters and immigration lawyers

With 100s of stories to back it all up. 

Now let us talk about LinkedIn, because this is where most of you silently lose the game.

[4] Your banner is free marketing space and most of you waste it with random university photos, stock images, or motivational quotes. 

When someone visits your profile, they should immediately understand three things without scrolling. What role you want. What skills you use. What kind of problems you solve. If that is not visible in five seconds, you lose attention.

[5] Your headline is not for creativity. It is for visibility.

Recruiters do not search feelings. They search keywords. Software Engineer. Data Analyst. Product Manager. SQL. Python. PowerBI. AWS. React. 

If your headline does not contain the actual job titles and tools you want to be found for, you simply will not appear in searches. No visibility means no inbound opportunities.

I have spoken to 500+ job seekers and today run a community of 4,800+ people inside Job Hunting Accelerator. And if there is one thing that quietly destroys more careers than rejection itself, it is comparison.

Some people in my community got hired after 50 to 60 applications.
Some crossed 1,000 applications before landing their first offer.

Both are real.
Both are normal.
And both mess with your head in very different ways.

The moment you see someone land an offer with 60 applications while you are sitting at 400, your mind starts spiraling.

“What am I doing wrong?”
“Am I not good enough?”
“Why is this taking me so long?”

I get it.
I have been there too.

But you need to understand:

– You can only control your method.
– You can only control your process.
– You cannot control timing or outcomes.

Job hunting is not a race with equal starting lines.

Different resumes.
Different networks.
Different backgrounds.
Different luck windows.
Different visa situations.

Two people can follow the same strategy and still get very different results.

That does not make one smart and the other broken.
It just means the market responded differently.

Where people lose the plot is when they start measuring their journey using someone else’s speed.
That is when confidence starts cracking.
That is when consistency drops.
That is when people quit too early.

Job hunting is not about fast.
It is about persistence.

And the ones who eventually win are not always the ones with the cleanest trajectory.
They are the ones who refused to stop just because the timeline offended their expectations.

Do not let someone else’s early win convince you that you are late.

You are not late.
You are still in the process.

--- Post 171 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Oct 16, 2025
Engagement: 98 reactions, 8 comments, 3 reposts (Score: 576)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7382082485876207616

Give me 2 minutes and I’ll give you back a cold email strategy that has helped international students land offers from Meta, AMD, Tesla, Nvidia & Capital One

I’ve shot over 100+ podcasts with the best of the best international students in the last 6 months, and this is exactly how they reached out to people, which led to great opportunities for them. 

Step 1 // Know exactly who to reach out to (and why it matters)

Most students get this part wrong and end up wasting time. Instead of blasting emails to recruiters or senior execs who never respond, take time to identify 10–15 people per company who are 1 or 2 levels above the job you want.

For example, if you’re aiming for a Software Engineer 1 role, look for SDE-2s, Senior Engineers, or Team Leads.

Step 2 // Become genuinely interested in their work (don’t fake it)

You can spot a “template” email from a mile away, so can they.
Spend at least 10–15 minutes reading their LinkedIn posts, GitHub repos, or old talks.

Check message examples in the comments to get more insight. 

Note something specific: a project they shipped, an open-source tool they built, or an idea from their writing you liked. When you reference this in your message, it immediately sets you apart as someone who actually cares.

Step 3 // Find their email (not just their LinkedIn)

Most people stop at DMs. That’s why they never get through. To actually land in their inbox, use tools like Hunter.io, Apollo, or RocketReach to dig up their email.

If these don’t work, try common email patterns like firstname.lastname@company.com or firstinitiallastname@company.com.

And always double-check, Hunter or NeverBounce can help verify.
Landing in the inbox is half the battle.

Step 4 // Write like a human, not a script generator

Start with a simple subject line and in your first 2 lines, mention the thing you genuinely liked about their work.

Get to the point quickly. State who you are, what you’re aiming for, and why you’re reaching out to them (not anyone else). People appreciate clarity and honesty over polish.

Step 5 // Track every interaction, every follow-up

Don’t let your outreach disappear into the void. Create a simple Google Sheet and list every name, company, role, email date, and follow-up status.

Most successful students were methodical, they knew who replied, who bounced, and when to follow up. Treat your job hunt like a campaign, not a shot in the dark.

Step 6 // Persistence beats luck, every single time

The uncomfortable truth? Most replies won’t come from the first email.
80% of positive responses happened after a second or even third follow-up.

Wait 4–5 days after your first email, then nudge them again, short, polite, and with context.

Cold emails are not magic.
But they’re a proven edge if you do them right.

============================================================

They said "Marry an American and you'll get your citizenship easily." That's a lie.

I've survived both employment and marriage-based green card processes, and here's the reality.

Yes, getting a green card through marriage is faster, usually one to two years. If you're from India like me, that's literally a lifesaver because otherwise you're in the employment-based backlog, which is 50+ years long.

But it's not easy. First, you file Form I-130. Then you file Form I-485 to adjust your status if you're already in the United States (which I was, on an H-1B visa).

Then you have to hand over your entire life summary to the government showing evidence of your love and your marriage.

Photos together. Texts. Joint bank accounts. Lease agreements. Utility bills. Anything that proves your relationship is real and not just for immigration benefit.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. This question came up, and the answer surprised many people. 

Also, if you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

Now here's the scary part: USCIS will interview you before giving you a green card.

Sometimes they separate husband and wife into two separate rooms to ask questions.

If your stories don't align, if one of you says you got married at the courthouse and the other says it was at a venue, if one says your first date was at a restaurant and the other says it was a movie, your petition gets rejected right there.

They ask things like:
- What side of the bed does your spouse sleep on?
- What did you have for breakfast this morning?
- What toothpaste does your spouse use?
- When was the last time you had an argument and what was it about?

They're looking for inconsistencies that prove the marriage isn't real. Marriage-based green card is faster than the India EB backlog.

But it's not the easy shortcut people think it is.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is based on my personal experience. If you're considering marriage-based adjustment of status, speak with a qualified immigration attorney about your specific situation.

"I lost my H-1B job in January 2024. I'm still in the US. My I-94 says I'm good until February 2026. Should I switch to B-2 or just go back?"

This question came up on our Wednesday livestream and the answer just broke me. ( Context: I have a livestream with an immigration lawyer from Manifest Law on my channel )

The immigration lawyer's response: "You've been overstaying since March 2024. If you leave the country now, you might be inadmissible for 10 years."

Let me explain how this happens, because I think a lot of people might find themselves in this situation unintentionally. 

➤ The Timeline of the person who asked the question:
- January 2024: Lost H-1B job
- March 2024: 60-day grace period ends
- January 2025: Still in US, no job, no status change filed
- February 2026: I-94 expiration date

They looked at their I-94 and thought: "I'm legal until February 2026."

They're not.
Your H-1B status requires you to work for your sponsor.

When you lose your job, you get 60 days to:
- Transfer to a new H-1B employer, OR
- Change status (F-1, H-4, B-2), OR
- Leave the country

If you do nothing? Your status ends after 60 days.
Not when your I-94 expires. After 60 days.
After that, every day you stay = unlawful presence.

➤ The 10-Year Ban

US immigration law is brutal:

180 days of overstaying = 3-year entry ban
1 year of overstaying = 10-year entry ban
This person lost their job in January 2024.

By March 2024, grace period ended.
By January 2025, they'd overstayed for nearly a year.

If they leave now, they trigger the 10-year ban.
Your I-94 shows how long you can stay based on your visa stamp. But that's only valid if you're maintaining status.

H-1B status = actively employed.
No job = no status = I-94 date means nothing.

This isn't like F-1 where you have "duration of status" protection.

H-1B doesn't work that way. I hope you're staying safe and educating yourself, please do not make this mistake. 

--
P.S: I send out a weekly newsletter on immigration, job-hunting, and resources. Join 35,000+ readers for free: https://lnkd.in/evyieG-h

--

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only. If you've lost your H-1B job or think you might be out of status, talk to a qualified immigration lawyer immediately.

Author: priteshjagani

You got H-1B selected and you want to go to India. Before you book that ticket, there are three things you need to know first.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with an immigration lawyer from Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. This week, someone asked if they could travel to India after getting selected in the H-1B lottery. 

The answer is more complicated than most people expect. 

There's a common misconception here. Getting registered in the lottery is not the same as filing. Your employer has a window after selection to actually submit the petition to USCIS. 

During that window, travel isn't automatically off the table. But it's not automatically safe either.

Btw, If you're navigating H-1B questions after lottery selection, grab a 30-min free consultation with Manifest Law to discuss your specific situation: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

The lawyer explained that the moment you actually need to stay grounded is when your employer is ready to file and send the application. Not from the day you find out you're selected. Only when the paperwork is about to go out.

That said, a few things to note before you book anything:

[1] If you're on F-1 or OPT, do not travel.

If your employer filed a Change of Status petition, traveling abroad is considered abandoning that request. You won't automatically transition to H-1B on October 1. You'd be stuck outside the US until you get a visa stamp and your employer may have to refile under consular processing.

[2] Visa stamping backlogs in India are severe.

US consulates in India are severely backlogged. Even if your petition gets approved, getting an interview slot could take months. If you travel assuming you'll be back in time, that assumption could strand you.

[3] Consistency rules are strict this cycle.
The wage level, job code, and work location on your final petition must match your lottery registration. Any changes your employer makes before filing, including remote work arrangements while you're abroad, could create issues.

The short answer: if you have a valid H-1B visa stamp and your petition is for consular processing, travel may be fine. For everyone else, talk to an attorney before booking anything.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only based on my conversation with an immigration lawyer. Travel decisions during H-1B processing depend heavily on your specific visa status and situation. Consult a qualified immigration attorney before making any travel plans.

This international student came to the US in 2024, in one of the toughest markets for jobseekers.

Within her first year at Purdue University Master of Engineering Management Program (MEM), she had already landed a Production and Ops internship at Panasonic that got extended into a co-op.

This is Paridha Talmale’s story.

At first, she did what most students do.

She went straight into the volume game.
 - Applied a lot, across roles that sounded “close enough”
 - Assumed more applications meant more chances
 - Took rejections personally, even when the role was never a real fit

Very quickly, she noticed a painful truth.

If you keep doing only quantity, you start losing confidence faster than you gain opportunities.

Things shifted when she rebuilt her approach with intention.

She stopped applying based on job titles and started applying based on job descriptions.

“Production Engineering” sounded intimidating, but when she read the actual JD, it matched her mechanical background and her MEM coursework.

Her rule became simple:

My job is to apply.
Their job is to reject.
I will not self-reject.

She switched from “apply and wait” to “apply and follow up.”

Panasonic came through a normal application, but she realized the only way to stand out was to show real interest.

So she did the uncomfortable work:
 - Found the recruiter’s email
 - Followed up after her video interview
 - Reached out to people inside Panasonic, including former interns who converted full-time
 - Stayed visible without being pushy

That follow-up sequence is what helped her get the hiring manager round scheduled.

She learned what networking actually is.

Not asking for referrals.
Not sending “please help” messages.

Her approach:
 - Message people in the same role she’s applying for
 - Lead with curiosity: “How does this role really work day to day?”
 - Learn the language of the job so her interviews sounded like she belonged there
 - Build trust first, opportunity later

She used career fairs the right way.
She walked into Purdue’s career fairs and conferences knowing two things:
Big brand lines are endless

Smaller booths can have real, human conversations
She treated career fairs like relationship-building, not instant hiring.

One good conversation can become a door later.

A big part of her progress also came from the Purdue MEM ecosystem.
- Resume reviews and mock interviews with the career team
- Guidance on picking coursework that builds a coherent story
- On-campus projects that give real experience, not just “classwork” bullets

Her bigger takeaway is the one most students learn too late:

Your master’s is not a single task.

It’s a full journey.

If you never protect your mental and physical health, the job hunt starts breaking you.

How you do anything is how you do everything in a job search.

And nothing proved it to me more clearly than observing international students go from 0 callbacks for months to landing internships and jobs at AMD, Confluent, Google, Tesla, Capital One, and many great companies. 

Because the small things they did every day during the job hunt set them up for success for their job as well. 

How you react to rejection
→ Tells the interviewer how you’ll handle pressure when deadlines explode.

How you rewrite your resume after 30 days of silence
→ Shows whether you improve your craft or keep repeating the same mistakes.

How you reach out to 3 people instead of blasting 300 cold messages
→ Reveals the clarity you bring to networking.

How you study a company before applying
→ Reflects the depth you’ll bring into stakeholder conversations later.

How you show up on bad days
→ Predicts whether you’ll survive the emotional rollercoaster of tech interviews.

How you talk about your past work
→ Signals if you actually understand what you did or if you’re just listing buzzwords.

How you choose who to learn from
→ Shows whether you follow noise or seek relevance.

I’ve seen many journeys that prove this. 

Like Samhita Irrinki, who went from months of 0 callbacks to AMD by mapping her past work to the semiconductor world, building custom decks, learning from TPMs in her network, and applying with intention instead of praying and spraying.

Like Prasanna Srinivasan Gorty from Georgia Tech, who started applying from India months before landing, traded a dining hall job for a supply chain RA, walked into a career fair with a focused list, and still came out in the worst market with 3 offers in 3 months.

Like Namita Manage from Northeastern, who sent 50 to 60 applications a day in Boston, tracked them in a sheet, kept tailoring her resume after each dry month, cold emailed recruiters while friends were getting offers, and kept doing mock interviews until one full time offer landed.

Your job search is built on small moments and habits that add up. 
It’s built on how you behave in the boring, invisible parts of the process.

Because if you cut corners in the hunt, you’ll cut corners in the role.
But if you build discipline in the hunt, you carry that discipline into the role.

How you do anything is how you do everything.
And hiring teams notice it long before you walk into the room.

My first birthday in America shocked me.

I had moved to the U.S. 11 years ago as a student. Tight budget. Every dollar mattered.

On my birthday, my on-campus manager asked,
“Where do you want to go for lunch?”

My brain didn’t think celebration.
It thought calculation.

How much will this cost?
How many people are coming?
Will I have to pay for everyone?

At the restaurant, I was still anxious. When the bill came, I reached for my wallet.

He stopped me.

“It’s your birthday. We are treating you.”

That’s when I learned something simple but powerful:

In India, you treat others on your birthday.
In America, people treat you.

Small cultural shifts like this teach you more than any classroom ever could.

What cultural difference surprised you the most when you moved?

These 50 DSA Patterns and 200+ problems helped software engineers in my community land offers at Amazon, Google, and Meta in the worst job market of 2025..

No matter which big tech company you apply to
No matter what designation you have SDE-1, 2, 3

The process will always start with a DSA round, which is the preliminary round that ensures you go forward in the hiring process and eventually land the offer. 

There are about 50 Patterns that you can study, depending on where you face problems and the company you want to work for.

Author: priteshjagani

"My STEM OPT ends in July. If my H-1B gets selected and approved, what happens after July if my OPT has already expired? Am I allowed to stay and work until October 1st, or do I fall out of status in between?"

This is one of the biggest sources of panic for people waiting for H-1B results.

The answer: You're covered. It's called the cap gap.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. 

Also, If you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

Cap gap is the grace period that bridges the time between when your OPT/STEM OPT ends and when your H-1B officially starts on October 1st.

➤ Can You Work During Cap Gap?

Yes. This is how It Works:

Let's say your STEM OPT ends July 15, 2026.

Your H-1B doesn't start until October 1, 2026.

That's a 2.5-month gap.

Cap gap protection means:

- You can stay in the United States legally during that period
- You can continue working for your employer during that period
- You don't fall out of status

➤ What You Need To Show Your Employer:

Your H-1B approval notice. That approval notice extends your work authorization through September 30, 2026. Your employer can verify this and keep you on payroll during the gap period.

➤ When Does Cap Gap Apply?

Cap gap applies when:

- Your OPT or STEM OPT expires before October 1st
- You have a pending or approved H-1B petition that starts October 1st
- You timely filed the H-1B before your OPT ended

This is why immigration lawyers emphasized on previous calls: "File your H-1B change of status BEFORE your OPT ends, even if the filing window goes until June 30th."

If you file after your OPT expires, you don't get cap gap protection.

➤ What If Your H-1B Gets Denied?

If your H-1B gets denied during the cap gap period, your cap gap protection ends, and you would need to leave the US or change to another status.

But if it's approved, you're covered all the way through September 30th, and then your H-1B activates October 1st.

➤ Common Employer Question:

Some employers get nervous when they see your OPT EAD card has an expiration date before October 1st. They ask: "Can you legally work between your OPT end date and October 1st?"

The answer is yes, as long as you have an approved H-1B petition.

Show them the H-1B approval notice (I-797). That's the documentation they need.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only based on conversations with immigration lawyers. If you have questions about cap gap protection, speak with a qualified immigration attorney.

This software engineer automated his job search with Claude and turned the most draining part of the process into a review-and-approve workflow.

Here’s how it works:

1) What this tool actually does

a) He used a repo called Shortlist MCP that connects Claude to job application systems.
b) Claude can search for jobs, queue them up, prefill forms, and submit them after review.
c) The point is not to let AI think for you. The point is to stop wasting time on copy-paste work.

2) What you need before it works

a) You need Claude Code, Claude Desktop, or another MCP-compatible client.
b) You need a Shortlist account with your resume, profile, and basic details already saved.
c) You need your information cleaned up first, because good automation still depends on good inputs.

3) How the setup works

a) You connect Claude to the Shortlist MCP server once.
b) After login, Claude can search jobs by role, company, and location.
c) You can also paste job links directly and add the ones you want into a queue.

4) What happens during the application

a) The system reads the application form and maps the fields.
b) It fills in the repeated details using your saved profile and resume.
c) For written answers, Claude drafts a response, you review it, and only then does anything get submitted.

5) Why this is useful, and where people should be careful

a) This is great for removing the boring 80% of job applications like dropdowns, uploads, repeated fields, and basic screening forms.

b) It is not a replacement for judgment, especially for roles you really care about. Those still need a human pass.

c) The smart way to use this is to automate the friction, then use your energy on networking, interview prep, and tailoring the few applications that matter most.

AI is not automatically getting people hired. It is simply removing low-value work so candidates can spend more time on the parts that actually move the needle.

Author: priteshjagani

We’ve all heard on LinkedIn this year that:
– Amazon cut 30,000 roles
– Accenture cut 11,000
– Salesforce cut 4,000
– Oracle cut 3,000

After 8 years in tech, living in California, meeting engineers, PMs, managers, and directors across these companies, here’s a harsh reality most people on the outside never get to see.

A big chunk of layoffs has nothing to do with the economy. The cracks start inside the organization long before a single employee gets a calendar invite from HR.

Here’s what actually happens behind the scenes.

It often starts when a senior leader hears something like:
You need more impact to be considered for your next level.

This flips a switch.

1. The race for bigger teams begins
 To show broader influence, they start pulling people into their org. Headcount becomes the shortcut to visibility.

2. The workload gets stretched thin
Work that used to be handled by one sharp engineer is now spread across three. Nobody is working at full utilization, but the team keeps growing.

3. Ownership becomes a battlefield
Since the work isn’t deep enough for everyone, people start scrambling for whatever looks important. More politics. Less progress.

4. More steps get added to look structured
Instead of faster delivery, leaders add review cycles and status meetings. The team looks bigger on paper, but output slows down.

5. The leader eventually gets their level up
And usually moves to another group. Leaving behind an oversized team held together by work that never justified its size.

So when budget cuts hit, these ballooned orgs are the first ones flagged.

Not because the employees are weak.
Not because the business suddenly collapsed.
But because the structure was unstable from the start.

From the outside, layoffs look random.
From the inside, it’s painfully predictable.

And the saddest part?
Most people who lose their jobs never realize they were part of a team that was built only to serve someone else’s career ladder.

Until promotions are tied to real outcomes instead of headcount, this cycle won’t stop.

So if you’ve been unemployed for months and you’re blaming yourself, pause.
A lot of good people are paying for decisions they never got to influence.

You didn’t mess up.
The system is messy.

This international student got laid off while on OPT, had only 90 days to land a job, but got hired in just 40 days as a Product Manager. Meet Aman, who graduated from CMU and is currently an Agentic AI PM.

This is how he beat the OPT clock without depending on mass applying.

Aman got laid off with a same-day termination. The first mistake most people make is that they start “applying harder” with zero system. He did the opposite. He built a system first, then executed fast.

Btw, before we get into it, I totally get that job-hunting in 2026 is extremely tiring, long, and painful. The pressure, anxiety, and stress of getting ghosted & rejected add up quickly, not to mention it now takes 500-1000 applications for 1 offer.

That's why if this market is draining you and you want structured help on resume, outreach, and strategy, you can explore my Job Hunting Accelerator: 5,100+ people are already using it to land offers.

Link: https://lnkd.in/gt5YBAbr

1. He paused the clock before chasing offers

Within the first week, he found a legit way to stop the unemployment timer by getting a role with a professor (even a volunteer ML role can help, depending on your case).

Action: In the first 72 hours, email 20 professors or labs with one line: “I can help with X for 10 hrs/week. Here is my resume. Can we speak?”

Target: 20 emails, 5 follow-ups, 1 yes.

2. He built a “Referral Router” in Excel

He created a sheet with 80–90 names and two key columns: Company and “Who else can intro me.”

Action: Build this in 60 minutes tonight:

- Column A: Name
- B: Company
- C: What team / domain
- D: How I know them
- E: Who they can connect me to

Target: 50 names minimum in week 1. Add 10 names every week forever.

3. He stopped asking “Are you hiring?” and started asking for a name

Most networking fails because people ask lazy questions.

Action: DM your closest contact at a target company with this:

“Hey, quick question. Who is the hiring manager or recruiter for the [Role Title] opening? I’m applying today and want to send a direct note.”

Target: 5 such messages per day.

4. He used the hiring manager's email

He got the hiring manager’s name, used Hunter/Apollo to find the email, and sent a 3-line note that does not feel needy.

Template:

Subject: [Role] | [Your niche]

Line 1: “I’m a CMU grad with X years in [domain], recently shipped [result/metric].”

Line 2: “This role needs [job requirement]. I’ve done that via [proof].”

Line 3: “Can I send you a 1-page snapshot of my work, and would you be open to a 10-min screen if it’s relevant?”

Target: 10 emails/day. Follow up twice (Day 3 and Day 7).

5. He avoided “general PM” and picked one lane

He focused on Agentic AI PM because his edge was engineering + AI depth.

Action: Write your lane in one sentence and reject anything outside it for 30 days:

“I am targeting [lane] roles in [industry], because my proof is [2 bullets].”

Target: 30 high-fit applications beats 300 random ones.

Last week, inside my job-hunting community, I reviewed 17 resumes & 15 had the exact problem that was holding them back. 

It is the same thing I’ve seen in 100s of resumes as a Hiring Manager when I used to hire for my team, this included designers, engineers, and other roles. 

People usually describe responsibility.
Not impact.

And recruiters do not scan for responsibility.
They scan for proof.

First, the ATS scans for keywords.
Then a human scans for numbers.
Then they decide if you can create impact.

Btw, if you’re a jobseeker, I totally understand that job-hunting in 2026 is extremely tiring and painful. The pressure, anxiety, and stress of getting ghosted & rejected add up quickly, not to mention it now takes 500-1000 applications for 1 offer.

That's why if this market is draining you and you want structured help on resume, outreach, and strategy, you can explore my Job Hunting Accelerator: 5,100+ people are already using it to land offers. 

Link: https://lnkd.in/gt5YBAbr

Here are 5 types of proof almost every job can show.

1. Revenue generated

You do not need to work in sales to show revenue. If something you did helped people spend more, renew more, expand more, or not churn, there is money attached to it.

Think: additional revenue, uplift in conversion, ARPU, LTV, upsell, cross sell.

2. User growth

Companies care about users who show up, activate, and stay.

If your work:
- brought more qualified users in
- helped them reach first value faster
- made them stick for longer

then you have growth impact.

Think: signups, MAU, DAU, activation rate, retention, adoption and engagement.

3. Cost savings

In this market, saving money is as powerful as making it.

Maybe you:
- reduced cloud bills or tool spend
- cut manual work so a team could do more with the same headcount
- removed an old feature or vendor that was burning money

Think: reduced spend, fewer tickets, fewer errors, less manual effort, smaller teams doing the same work.

4. Time reduction

Time is money and speed is a competitive edge.
Anything that makes work faster for customers or for teams is real impact:

- faster pages and APIs
- faster reports
- faster deployments
- faster decision cycles

Think: before vs after timelines, hours saved per week, release frequency.

5. Team size and scope

Scope shows what level of responsibility you can handle.
Signals like these matter a lot:

- how many people you worked with or led
- how many services, regions, or products you owned
- how many users or dollars your work touched

Scope tells a hiring manager if you are ready for bigger bets.

- Reorder bullets
- And make every line answer one question:
What changed because of me?

If you’re going to spend ₹50 Lakhs just to 'explore your options' in the US, don’t come!!

USA is literally dead, that’s the sentiment I heard everywhere in India when I wrapped up my study in the USA workshops back in November. 

And I don’t blame students for feeling this way. If you’re considering studying in the USA, here’s my honest take after meeting 1000+ students just a few weeks back in India. 

Group 1: “I am all in on the US.”
You know you want to build your career in the US.
You have admits, you have a plan, you are ready for the grind.
You are not asking “Should I go?”
You are asking “How do I make this work?”

You should go. For you, the US is a calculated risk, not a blind bet.

Group 2: “I have decided I am not going.”
You have looked at the market, the visa drama, the cost, the uncertainty.
You have decided to pause, build your profile in India, and revisit the decision later.

You should stay. That is also a strong decision, not a failure.

Group 3: “I am confused, but the loan is ready.”

You are earning decent money in India or just finished your degree.
You feel stuck in the current market, so a US Master’s looks like a reset button.

You think, “Let me just go. I will figure it out there.”

This is who I am talking to. If you are about to spend ₹50 Lakhs for “clarity,” please slow down.

Because here is what I am seeing right now in the US.

I visited 10 to 15 universities this year.
I recorded podcasts with 50 to 60 students and graduates.
Almost all of them arrived in the same brutal job market.

Some of them cracked jobs within months of landing.
Some of them are at the end of their OPT year, still searching for that first full-time role.

Same country. 
Same market. 
Completely different outcomes.

The difference was not luck alone.
The difference was how they came.

If you are about to take a collateral loan, or your family is betting years of savings on you, ask yourself a few direct questions:

– Do I know the role I am targeting, or am I hoping the degree will “figure it out” for me ?
– Do I have at least some relevant experience, projects, or internships connected to that role ?
– Do I have the emotional and financial capacity to handle a year of uncertainty while I work towards that first job ?
– Am I already doing the work today, in India, that a strong candidate in the US would do?

If most of your answers are yes, then the US can still be an incredible accelerator for you, even in a tough market.

If most of your answers are “I don’t know yet,” then the US is not a bad idea. It is just not a “right now” idea.

Use the next 6 to 12 months to build clarity, experience, and a job hunt strategy. Then spend the ₹50 Lakhs as an investment, not as a very expensive experiment.

–
P.S: If you are in Group 1, 2 or 3, drop a comment and share where you are right now. Your story or opinion might be the exact clarity someone else needs before they sign that loan form.

Author: priteshjagani

The Salary negotiation framework I know now that I wish I knew at 24 while having 3 offers on the table and picking my first as a Product Manager...

1. Mindset: you are closing a business deal

By the time you get an offer, the company has already spent weeks or months on you. Shortlisting, interviews, internal discussions, approvals.

So you are not a random resume. 
You are a solution to a very real problem.

Remember this:

– Not negotiating is also a decision. It just happens to be the most expensive one.
– A respectful negotiation rarely gets offers revoked.
– You are not asking for charity. 
– You are pricing a business outcome.

Go into the conversation with that energy. 

2. Get crystal clear on the value you are about to create

Before you talk numbers, get this from the hiring manager:
In the first 12 months, what does success look like in this role?

Push till you have 4 to 6 clear outcomes, for example:
– Launch feature X in market Y
–  Improve signup conversion from 15% to 25%
–  Reduce ticket volume by 20% with better workflows
– Build a cross functional squad and hiring pipeline

Now build a simple grid for yourself:

–  Column 1: Goal
– Column 2: What it is worth for the business
+ More revenue
+ Lower churn
+ Less operational cost
+ Lower risk
– Column 3: What you will do to make it happen
– Column 4: Evidence from your past that you can do it

You are now ready to say:

"I understand you want A, B, C from this role. Here is how I plan to achieve that and why I am confident, based on what I have done before."

This is the foundation of your negotiation. 

3. Do your homework on the market

Now you figure out what a fair salary looks like for that value.

Use tools like:
– Levels.fyi for band and comp structure at larger companies
– Glassdoor and similar sites for ranges and company specific data
– Job descriptions that mention salary bands
– Conversations with people in the same role and level

From this you create three numbers:

– Dream number: what you would love to get
– Fair target: what feels right for your level and market
– Walk away: the number below which it simply does not make sense

Your goal is to negotiate toward the fair or dream number and have the courage to walk away if they are stuck below your minimum.

4. The rule for what to actually say

When they give you the offer:

1. Thank them. Ask for 24 to 48 hours to review everything.
2. Revisit your grid and market research.
3. Come back with a specific, justified ask.

Keep it simple, for example:
"Thank you again for the offer. I am excited about the role and the problems we discussed.

I did some homework on similar Product Manager roles in this market and the range I see for this scope is around X range. With my experience shipping X and Y and the outcomes we spoke about, I was hoping we could be closer to Y in total compensation.

Is there any room to move in that direction?"

Check comments for the rest of the breakdown:

Author: priteshjagani
Date: Feb 13, 2026
Engagement: 75 reactions, 7 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 447)

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7422428282307989504

The h-1B lottery is no longer a random 1-in-4 shot. In 2026, you can literally triple your entries by making one strategic move: changing your zip code.

Here is how a Software Engineer in rural Georgia is now 3x more likely to get picked than the same engineer in San Francisco.

Context: Every Wednesday, I sit down with an immigration lawyer on my YouTube channel. This is based on our conversation. I'll be having another livestream this Wednesday as well, do join :) 

1. The lottery is now wage-level heavy

Under the new system:
- Your H1B registration is tied to your wage level
- Wage level depends on

+ Your role (SOC code)
+ Your work location (county)
+ The salary your employer is willing to pay

Wage Level 1 gets 1 entry
Wage Level 3 gets 3 …. and level 4 salary gets 4 entries in the lottery.
So the game is no longer “one person, one ticket”.

It is “your wage level decides how many tickets you get”.

2. Same job, same company, different county, completely different odds

Example we discussed:

- Software Engineer role
- Same SOC code
- Same company

Option A: You sit in a very expensive, urban county like San Francisco

- High prevailing wage
- Your salary might only qualify as Wage Level 1 or 2
- That means 1 or 2 entries in the lottery

Option B: You are hired to work remotely from a rural county in Georgia

- The prevailing wage is lower there
- The same salary can qualify as Wage Level 3
- That gives you 3 entries in the lottery

Nothing changed in your skills.
Only your work location changed.
Your odds of selection just multiplied.

3. What this means for you before March

If you are on F1, OPT, STEM OPT, or another status and planning to file H1B this year:

- Do not ignore the county your LCA will use. If your role can be remote or hybrid, talk to your employer about working from a lower-cost county
- Ask them and their attorney what wage level your offer falls into for that location
- Understand clearly
+ SOC code
+ County
+ Wage level

Also, this is not hacking the system. It is about understanding the rules and aligning your job setup with them.

4. What you should not do

- Do not push your employer to lie about your location just to get a higher wage level
- Work location must match reality.

FDNS site visits and compliance checks are very real and can cause serious issues if your paperwork and real life do not match

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This post is for educational purposes only and is based on my public conversation with an immigration lawyer. Your case may be very different. Before you decide on location, wages, or H1B strategy, speak with a qualified immigration professional who can review your specific situation.

--

Here’s how it happens:
- Apps like Wise or Remitly show you “low fees,” but the bigger cut is quietly added inside the exchange rate.

- Google shows you the mid-market rate, but the actual rate you get is lower once you transfer.

- A $25 loss per transaction might not feel like much… until you realize that’s $300 gone in a year if you’re sending money home monthly.

And the truth is - we plan carefully for rent, tuition, groceries, and even taxes.
But we don’t stop to ask, “Am I actually getting the best value when I send money back home?”

Most immigrants think that getting the green card means they're finally safe in America. They're completely wrong. This is the truth you don’t know about: 

You actually have to renew your green card every 10 years. If it's conditional (like marriage-based in the first 2 years), you have to renew it every 2 years.

You have to think twice before taking long trips outside the United States. You'll be questioned when you come back. Stay out too long and you risk abandoning your permanent residence.

And yes, if you make any major legal mistakes, you can still be deported and your green card can be revoked. But the moment you naturalize and get your US passport, the game completely changes.

Btw, every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with Manifest Law on my YouTube channel, and this was another topic that came up. 

Also, if you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

Being a citizen means deportation over immigration status is no longer a concern. It's much harder to revoke your naturalization, nearly impossible compared to losing a green card.

You get permanent security. You get to travel hassle-free without worrying about how long you've been outside the US. And now you can sponsor your parents and siblings to come to America.

I'm working on my naturalization application right now. After years of visa uncertainty, F-1, OPT, H-1B, green card, I want the peace of mind that comes with citizenship.

Here's What Changes With Citizenship:

Green card: Renew every 10 years, can be revoked, travel restrictions, still deportable for certain offenses

Citizenship: No renewal, no deportation over immigration status, travel freely with US passport, sponsor immediate family

If you have your green card and you're eligible to naturalize (usually after 5 years, or 3 years if married to a US citizen), it's worth considering. I know the naturalization process feels like another hurdle after everything you've already been through.

But this is the last one. After this, you're done worrying about your immigration status forever.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is based on my personal experience. If you're considering naturalization or have questions about green card maintenance, speak with a qualified immigration attorney.

Author: priteshjagani

Engagement: 73 reactions, 4 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 407)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7429213252590600192

Most RFEs work like this: Government asks for a missing document → You submit it → Problem solved.

But E-Verify RFEs are a bit trickier, and I’ll tell you exactly why. 

You see, someone on our livestream asked, "I work for Walmart. I got an E-Verify RFE for my OPT. What should I submit?"

The immigration lawyer on my stream said, “I need to see your original filing. With E Verify, the officer checks whether your details were accurate on the date you filed, not just today.”

Context: Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with a lawyer from Manifest Law. 

Btw, If you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualift https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

➤ The Problem?

When you file OPT, you list your employer's E-Verify number and company name on your I 983 training plan. That information has to be accurate on the date you file.

If there is a mismatch now, USCIS will ask:

- Was the company actually E-Verified at that time
- Did the legal name on the form match the real hiring entity
- Is there enough evidence to show what was true when you filed

That is what your RFE response needs to clarify. RFEs exist so you can explain that story and submit proof. 

However, the stressful part is the waiting and the uncertainty, which puts a lot of stress on people.

➤ Why Walmart Got Flagged?

Walmart has multiple subsidiaries. Each can have its own E-Verify registration.

If your I-983 listed “Walmart Inc” but your actual offer letter says “Walmart Global Tech,” the system sees a mismatch. 

For you, it is all Walmart. 
For the officer, it is a different legal employer.

➤ The Stressful Scenario

Sometimes the problem is not the employee, it is the employer.

- The company thought it was E Verified, but never activated its account
- The E-Verify number on your form belongs to a different entity in the group
- HR reused an old template with the wrong details

In all of these, you respond with updated proof and an explanation. 

➤ What To Do If You Get An E-Verify RFE

Talk to an immigration attorney and have them review:

1. Your original I-983
2. Your offer letter and HR docs
3. The exact legal entity that hired you
4. Proof of the employer’s E-Verify status on your filing date

There is a big difference between “we just need clarification” and “the original filing had serious issues.” A lawyer can help you frame it correctly.

Do not assume. Big companies use many entities. Getting this right the first time is the easiest way to avoid this kind of RFE.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only. If you received an E-Verify RFE, speak with a qualified immigration attorney before responding.

Author: priteshjagani

Someone on our livestream thought they had the perfect plan to stay 16 years in the USA: "I'll do an MBA in the US after my L-1, get STEM OPT, then switch to H-1B for 6 more years."

The immigration lawyer's answer changed everything.

Here's what happened on the call:

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. This question came up, and the answer surprised many people.

Also, If you're thinking of applying for O-1, EB-1, and other talent-based visas, grab a 30-min free consultation & see if you qualify: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

The person asked: "I was on L-1 for 5 years. Now I'm converting to an F-1 for a Master's degree. After STEM OPT, how much H-1B time do I get?"

Nicole said:  "One year. L-1 and H-1B share the same 6-year clock. You already used 5 years on L-1”

You see, L-1 and H-1B don't each give you 6 years. 
They give you 6 years TOTAL across both visa types.

If you spend 5 years on L-1, you have 1 year of H-1B remaining.
If you spend 3 years on L-1, you have 3 years of H-1B remaining.
If you spend 6 years on L-1, you have 0 years of H-1B remaining.

The Math That Didn't Add Up:

The person thought:
- 5 years on L-1
- 2 years F-1 (MBA)
- 3 years OPT/STEM OPT
- 6 years H-1B
- Total: 16 years in the US

Nicole explained the reality:
- 5 years on L-1
- 2 years on F-1 (MBA)
- 3 years OPT/STEM OPT
- 1 year H-1B (because 5 years already used from the 6-year clock)
- Total: 11 years

Nicole said there's one way to reset the 6-year clock: Stay outside the US for 1 continuous year.

They asked: "What if I leave but come back on B-1/B-2 tourist visa for a few weeks?"

Nicole's answer: The tourist visits don't break the continuous requirement, but you still need to accumulate 365 days outside the US total.

What Nicole Recommended:

She said if you're on L-1 and considering this path, talk to an immigration attorney about:
- How much of your 6-year clock you've used
- How much H-1B time you'll actually have after OPT
- Whether your employer will sponsor your green card (EB-2/EB-3)
- Whether the reset option makes sense for your situation

Nicole emphasized: One year of H-1B might be enough if your employer starts your green card process immediately. But the calculation is important to understand before making the decision.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only based on my conversation with an immigration lawyer. If you're on L-1 and considering F-1 conversion, speak with a qualified immigration attorney about your specific situation.

A student asked us on the livestream: "I got Northeastern Boston with 10% scholarship and Syracuse with 50% scholarship. Which one should I pick?"

Here's the framework we used to help him think through it.

First: Both Are Good Universities

Let's be clear: Northeastern and Syracuse are both solid programs. You'll get a quality CS education at either. Companies recruit from both.

The comparison here is only about a fit for THIS student's specific situation.

➤ The Math He Was Looking At:

Northeastern Boston: ~$95,000-100,000 total (after 10% scholarship)

Syracuse: ~$50,000-55,000 total (after 50% scholarship)

Difference: $40,000-45,000

➤ The Framework We Discussed:

1. Financial Reality

If both universities cost the same after scholarships, Boston probably makes more sense, better location, stronger immediate access to the tech ecosystem.

But $40,000 is a strong gap. That's not just "a bit more expensive." That's half your total education cost.

2. Cohort Size & Opportunities

Northeastern's cohort in some programs: 1,000+ students

Syracuse: ~300 students

What this means practically:

- Smaller cohorts = better shot at TA/RA positions
- Professors more likely to know you personally
- Stronger recommendations become easier to get

This isn't a ranking thing.

It's a numbers thing.

3. Location Access

Boston has immediate access to the tech ecosystem. No question.

But Syracuse is 3-4 hours from NYC. Still close enough for career fairs, recruiting events, and networking trips when they matter.

4. ROI Consideration

Same companies recruit from both universities.

The degree outcome is comparable.

The question becomes: Is the Boston location worth $40,000 more for THIS student's situation?

What We Told Him:

"If you get Texas A&M, that's your top choice. Between Northeastern and Syracuse, given the scholarship difference and cohort size, Syracuse might give you better ROI. You're still close to NYC, a smaller cohort means better TA opportunities, and you graduate with significantly less debt."

When Northeastern Would Make More Sense:

- If scholarships were equal or closer
- If you already have a strong network in Boston to leverage
- If you got into their specialized co-op program

Again, this was For THIS student, with THESE scholarship amounts, in THIS financial situation, which path makes more sense?

The answer came down to debt load, cohort size, and practical ROI.

Your situation might be different.
Your decision might be different.

That's okay.

6 months left on your H-1B, and you think your only option is the lottery again. An immigration lawyer told me there are 3 paths most people never consider.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with an immigration lawyer from Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. Recently, someone asked what to do when they're almost at their 6-year H-1B limit. Join us this Wednesday for the next session.

Her answer surprised a lot of people on the call. Most H-1B holders approaching their limit assume it's over. Lottery again. Hope for the best. But she laid out three options that don't get talked about nearly enough.

Path 1: The O-1

Depending on your field, your work history, and where you're from, you might qualify for an O-1 visa. She said if you can find an employer willing to sponsor you, this is a real path worth exploring. It's not for everyone, but more people qualify than they think.

Path 2: H-1B Cap-Exempt

This was her favorite. The H-1B cap-exempt visa is not subject to the lottery. No April panic. No 3-in-10 odds. She said to look at universities and non-profit employers. Most people have never even heard of this option, and that's exactly the problem.

If you're navigating H-1B options with months left on your clock, grab a 30-min free consultation with Manifest Law to discuss what applies to your situation: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

Path 3: Travel to recapture time

This one is underused. She said every day you spend outside the US doesn't count toward your 6-year limit, which means you can get that time back. She told the story of a client who never traveled, never took a vacation, just ground through work.

She had to push him to go to Mexico, the Caribbean. He recaptured 4 to 5 months. That bought him enough time to file another extension and get everything sorted with USCIS.

Her advice: if you have a visa stamp, travel. Phone trips, work trips, vacation, it all counts.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only based on my conversation with an immigration lawyer. If you have 6 months or less remaining on your H-1B, consult a qualified immigration attorney to understand which options apply to your specific situation.

Now think about students and early professionals who send money home every month. Twelve transfers a year, and you have tipped your remittance app 250 to 300 dollars without even realizing it.

- Google shows the mid-market rate

- But the actual rate you get is slightly worse
- That gap is the fee

Author: priteshjagani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419780696220725248

99% of international students are unaware of this and are paying thousands of dollars in interest on their MS education loan. 

Taking an education loan in India to study in the US usually looks like this:

– You take a loan in INR at 10 to 13 percent, often with a parent as co-signer and your home as collateral.
– You move to the US, finish your degree, and start earning in dollars.
– Every month you convert USD to INR, fight forex rates, pay a variable interest rate and hope it stays “manageable.”
– You tell yourself that a higher salary will fix it someday.

Here is what most students forget:

– The loan is in INR, your income is in USD. Currency risk is on you.
– The rate in India is usually variable, so it can move up over time.
– Your parents stay legally and emotionally tied to your debt.
– Your repayments do not help your US credit history.
– You lose money in small forex fees and conversion markups every month.

This is where refinancing comes in.

Refinancing means a US lender pays off your Indian education loan and replaces it with a new loan in the US in your name. From that point:

– You pay in dollars from your US bank account.
– You can often move to a lower and fixed interest rate.
– Your repayments start building your US credit history.
– Your co-signer and collateral in India can be released.
– You avoid the monthly headache of sending money home.

Now, I know this is a very new topic for many international students, so let me address some common concerns out of the way. 

-> Is this some kind of loophole?
No. Refinancing is a standard product in the US for many types of loans. Here it is applied to education loans for international students.

-> Is the process painful?
Most of the work is one-time. For example, with MPOWER, you fill out an online form, upload documents, get a payoff letter from your Indian bank, and their team coordinates the rest with you. Most cases close in 10 to 20 days.

-> Can I prepay if I want to finish the loan early?
Yes. Education loans in the US typically do not have prepayment penalties, including MPOWER, so you can pay faster when your income grows.

Right now, MPOWER Financing is one of the only US-based lenders that focus specifically on international students and offer this refinance path for Indian education loans. 

This is not personal financial or tax advice from me. 
It is an option most people are never told about.

If you want to check whether refinancing could work for you, you can see your basic eligibility in under a minute here: https://lnkd.in/e2-pa7b9

Most students try to grow financially only by earning more.
The smarter ones also learn how to pay less for the same future.

Dear international students, if you’re looking for a job right now, let me remind you of the things that actually helped me get calls from Google, Meta, and LinkedIn:

You don’t need to apply to 300 random jobs a week.
→ What you do need is a resume that shows clear impact and results, not just a list of tasks.

You don’t need another 10 versions of your resume for every company.
→ You need ONE strong, metrics-driven resume and a LinkedIn that tells the same story.

You don’t need to join every job board or apply through every portal.
→ You need to spend time on referrals, DM insiders, join info sessions, attend hackathons, and build relationships with people already in the industry.

You don’t need to memorize a thousand LeetCode questions.
→ You need to master the core DSA patterns and understand the “why” behind each problem, focus on 100 well-chosen problems, not 1000 random ones.

You don’t need  to obsess over which programming language is “best.”
→ You need to get comfortable in one language and practice explaining your logic out loud, under real interview pressure.

You don’t need a perfect portfolio or fancy website from day one.
→ You need to show projects that solve burning problems, a bonus if they’re actually live and you can demo them.

You don’t need to wait until you feel “fully ready” before reaching out to people or applying.
→ You need to take action, get feedback, and iterate. Every day you’re silent, someone else is making a connection.

99% of the time, your biggest blocker is not skills, it’s actually not having a focused, feedback-driven plan.

So build the simplest strategy that works for you and execute it. 

Ship it.
Iterate.
Get feedback.
And repeat.

The only “best practice” at this stage is:
Stop chasing perfection.
Start chasing momentum.

And remember: almost every international student you admire felt invisible before they got that one “yes.”

and still hear,

“We went with someone else.”

Why?

Because job searching isn’t a formula.
It’s a game of probability.

Sometimes the company freezes hiring.
Sometimes an internal candidate pops up.
Sometimes the role shifts, budgets change, or timing just fails you.

And sometimes… they just choose someone else.

Not everything is in your control.

✅ Your preparation

from those who create their own luck.

But it’s also a rep in the gym of resilience.

Good.
That means you’re still in the arena.

Keep moving. Keep showing up.

Author: priteshjagani

Media: video
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7434741906712109056

Promotions in corporate America are not purely performance-based.

That’s the uncomfortable truth.

There’s a 3-level promotion rule:

Level 1: Performance
Doing your job well is the baseline. It keeps you employed.

Level 2: Visibility
Is your impact visible?
Do decision-makers know what you’re working on?

Level 3: Sponsorship
Is someone powerful advocating for you in rooms you’re not in?

Many immigrants stay mid-level for 8–10+ years because they optimize only for Level 1.

Hard work gets you hired.
Political capital gets you promoted.

If your name isn’t mentioned in leadership meetings, your promotion probability is low.

Are you building visibility and sponsorship or just working hard?

============================================================

There is no pain like watching your classmate crack a job in 90 days while you stare at an expiring visa after 365 days. I saw this exact scenario play out at 15 different universities in 2025. 

I watched some students sign an offer letter in Month 3, while their roommates packed their bags in Month 12. 

Same university. 
Same 'bad market'. 
Same degree. 

The difference was that one started preparing in India, and the other waited to land.
From everyone I met, three qualities kept showing up in the students who made it.

1. Career clarity before takeoff

“Something in tech or management” is not clarity.
“SDET at product companies” or “Data Analyst in healthcare analytics” is clarity.

Students who did well knew, even before they booked their tickets:

- What exact job titles they were targeting
- Their top 50 companies for those roles
- Which companies already hire from their university
- Where their alumni are working in those roles
- Who they wanted to talk to on LinkedIn before they landed

If you cannot answer “What role am I actually preparing for?” you are not ready for the job market. 

2. Focus when you land

The US will give you distractions on a platter.
Parties, roommate drama, trips, constant FOMO.
On top of that, money stress hits when the first semester bill arrives.

Many students come with the belief that an on campus job will cover living expenses.
In a lot of universities, that job is not guaranteed.

So people start working off campus because seniors say “It is fine, no one gets caught”
and suddenly most of their energy is going to survival, not career building.

Students who made it were very intentional:

- They knew exactly how they would cover the extra 1000 to 1200 dollars a month
- They picked on campus jobs that still left time and energy for internships and interviews
- They said yes to experiences that helped their profile, not every distraction that appeared

3. Preparation before the flight. 

The strongest students did not open LinkedIn seriously only after landing.

They were already in motion from India:
- Resume and LinkedIn aligned to their target roles
- Projects, research, or internships that directly supported those roles
- A basic job search system with target lists, outreach messages, and follow ups
- First layer of networking already started with alumni and seniors

So when they landed, they were not starting from zero.
They were already on step four or five.

Do not wait for the 365th day to realise you needed a plan on day 1.

Author: priteshjagani

20% of the habits successful job seekers have that give them 80% of the results.
This is the same thing I have been teaching to 4,800+ international students in the USA.

In 2024 everyone kept saying, “Worst job market. No one is hiring. Career fairs are useless.”

In the middle of that, Prasanna landed 3 internship offers within 90 days of landing in the US and now works at Micron in supply chain.

He did not get lucky.
He built the right habits.

Here are the ones that made the difference.

[1] CLARITY ABOUT ROLE

- He decided early that he wanted supply chain, not “anything in data or tech.”
- He shaped his resume to scream supply chain.
- He only applied to roles where his profile made sense.
 Result: Recruiters could understand his story in 5 seconds.

[2] START BEFORE YOU LAND

- He started applying in May, months before flying to the US.
- Early rejections helped him test his resume and build rejection tolerance.
- By the time he reached campus, he was already “warmed up” to the process.
 Result: No delay. He hit the ground running.

[3] USE UNIVERSITY LIKE AN ASSET

- He went after RA and lab work that matched supply chain.
- He turned hourly work into a funded GRA by doing good work and showing value.
- That GRA removed tuition, gave him a stipend, and made his resume stronger.
 Result: Less financial pressure, more relevant experience, more confidence.

[4] TREAT CAREER FAIRS LIKE A SYSTEM

- Before the fair he built a sheet of target companies and open roles.
- He applied online first, then used the fair to meet the right people.
- He did not walk to Micron first. He warmed up on lower priority booths, then went to his top choice once he felt ready.
- At Micron he asked for someone from the supply chain team, not a random recruiter.
 Result: Real conversation, not small talk. That one talk became his Micron offer.

[5] PERSISTENCE 

- He applied to 300 to 400 roles.
- He followed up 5 or 6 times with recruiters he met.
- He used simple follow up emails. No overthinking, no ego.
- He used rejection as data, not as a reason to stop.
 Result: 3 offers in 3 months in a year where many students got zero.

[6] PREP LIKE SOMEONE WHO EXPECTS TO GET INTERVIEWS

- He fed his resume and job description into ChatGPT and asked
 “If you were the hiring manager, what questions would you ask me”
- He prepared stories from his past work and lab projects.
- He studied the company products and the hiring manager’s background.
 Result: When calls came, he was ready. Not scrambling.

Get these 20% right, and you will see 80% of the results, even in a bad market.
- Clear target role
- Early start
- Strong university use
- Focused applications
- Consistent follow up
- Real interview prep

Author: priteshjagani

How to find your H1-B wage level in 5 steps and 45.79 seconds (if you are going to file H1-B this year, save this)

Your wage level affects:
- Whether you meet the prevailing wage
- How strong or weak your petition looks
- Your chances of getting RFEs

I am going to break this down as simply as possible so you can do it yourself. 

Quick note: This is for education, not legal, tax, or immigration advice. When in doubt, talk to an attorney or CPA who understands H1-B.

Step 1: Open the official DOL wage search

Go to: https://lnkd.in/g7pPGtEU

This is the OFLC Wage Search tool from the US Department of Labor.

Step 2: Select the correct data series and year

1. Under Data Series, pick the latest available wage data (usually the top or most recent year).
2. Leave Industry as All Industries.

You are looking at wages based on occupation and location, not a specific company or sector. This ensures you are using the most up-to-date wage numbers that USCIS will expect.

Step 3: Choose the right occupation using the SOC code

1. In the Occupation field, start typing your role or SOC code. Examples:
 - "Software Developers"
 - "Computer Systems Analysts"
 - "Financial Analysts"

2. Do not just pick something that "sounds close". Read the occupation description and match it based on what you actually do day to day.

3. If your employer or attorney gave you an SOC code, use that exact one.

What if your role is not listed?

Some positions, like Product Manager, do not have a direct match in the DOL database. If that happens:

1. Go to O*NET Online (onetonline.org), which is also a government resource.
2. Search for roles related to your job title.
3. Read through the listed tasks and responsibilities for each option.
4. The one that most closely matches your actual day-to-day work becomes your SOC code.

This step matters. Using the wrong SOC code can create inconsistencies that lead to RFEs.

Step 4: Enter your exact work location

Fill in:
1. State
2. County

Tips:
- Use the county where you physically work, not where the company headquarters is.
- If you work remotely, ask your attorney or HR how they are treating your worksite for H1-B purposes.

Step 5: Read the 4 wage levels for your role

A few key points that most people miss:

- Wage level is about the nature of the role, not "how generous" the salary is.
- Paying a high salary does not automatically make you Level 3 or 4 if your duties are still entry level.
- Filing a high wage level with low-level duties can make the case look fake or inconsistent.
- Filing a low wage level for a role that clearly expects senior-level responsibility can also raise questions.
- For H1-B purposes, USCIS is focused on your base salary, not total compensation with bonuses and stock.

--
P.S: I send out a weekly newsletter where I talk about immigration, job-hunting and resources for immigration as well. If you would like to join, it's absolutely free: https://lnkd.in/gce2ipwc

Author: priteshjagani

Engagement: 53 reactions (Score: 265)
Media: image

Rahul's OPT stamp got an RFE. His company was 100% real, he was sitting in their office when it happened. USCIS said his employer didn't exist in the E-Verify database.

Every Wednesday, I host an immigration Q&A with an immigration lawyer from Manifest Law on my YouTube channel. This week, we walked through a real RFE case that caught a lot of people off guard. Join us this Wednesday for the next session.

Here's what actually went wrong with Rahul’s case/ 

Rahul's offer letter and pay stub had his company's legal entity name. But his E-Verify number was registered under the parent company. To USCIS, that's a complete mismatch. And to make it worse, he had already switched to a new job by the time the RFE arrived.

This isn't a rare case. A lot of companies operate under a subsidiary name but run E-Verify under the parent. If nobody flags it during filing, USCIS will.

Btw, If you're navigating OPT, H-1B, or work visa questions, grab a 30-min free consultation with Manifest Law to discuss your options: https://lnkd.in/gbb3aTpa

So how do you respond without getting a denial?

The lawyer laid out two steps. First, go back to your old employer's HR and get a letter on official letterhead explaining that the parent company owns the subsidiary, and include the exact E-Verify number in that letter. 

Second, don't hide the new job. Show USCIS you have an updated I-20 and a new I-983 form. Trying to conceal the job switch at this stage is an instant denial.

The mismatch itself is fixable. The cover-up isn't.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This is for educational purposes only, based on my conversation with an immigration lawyer. If you've received an RFE related to E-Verify or employer verification, consult a qualified immigration attorney before responding.

============================================================

The new H-1B wage-based selection rule is FINAL, effective March 2026.

The rule shifts the lottery from random to wage-based.
Here’s the simplified model:

• Level 1 → 1 entry
• Level 2 → 2 entries
• Level 3 → 3 entries
• Level 4 → 4 entries

Higher wage = higher probability.
Master’s cap still applies.

This does NOT impact:
– Existing H-1B holders
– Cap-exempt H-1Bs

It only affects new lottery applicants in 2026 and beyond.

Key implications include:
• SOC code selection
• Wage level calculations
• Remote vs. onsite roles
• Employer compliance
• Salary strategy for OPT/STEM OPT students

I discussed all of this in detail with Manifest Law.

Dear h-1B Holders, If you don't HAVE to travel outside the USA, please don't.

h-1B holders are booking flights for a “quick trip” home, only to find their appointments pushed to December 2026. Your 2-week vacation can easily turn into a year outside the USA. Here’s what is happening with H1B stamping and travel right now.

Context: Every Wednesday, I sit down with an immigration lawyer on my YouTube channel. This is based on our conversation. (Link in Comments) 

➤ What we are seeing right now

- H1B visa appointments are getting rescheduled, often by many months
- Some cases are being pushed all the way to late 2026
- Extra social media vetting is causing longer security checks
- Very little clarity on when or how things will “go back to normal.”

➤ Simple rule:

- If you do not have to travel for stamping right now, avoid it
- If you already have a valid visa stamp and are only doing a short personal trip,
- Risk is lower, but you should still be careful and carry full documentation

➤ If you absolutely must travel for stamping, you should have a very strong reason, for example:

- Serious medical emergency in your immediate family
- A genuine financial hardship that you can show on paper
- Strong employer support for your travel and role

➤Then:

1) Book the earliest possible appointment, but be mentally ready for reschedules

2) Ask your employer to give you a detailed letter explaining your job, urgency, and need to return

3) Carry everything to the interview and at the port of entry

+ Approved petitions
+ Pay stubs
+ Employment letters
+ Any documents that support your emergency reason

4) If your appointment gets pushed very far, you can try requesting an expedited slot, but there is no guarantee it will be approved

For many people, the safest choice in this climate is simple: if the trip is optional, do not risk your status for a stamp.

Disclaimer: I am not an attorney. This post is for educational purposes only and is based on my public conversation with an immigration lawyer. Your situation may be very different. Before you make travel or stamping decisions, speak with a qualified immigration professional who can review your specific case.

--- Post 245 ---
Author: priteshjagani
Date: Mar 31, 2026
Engagement: 43 reactions, 1 comments (Score: 225)
Media: video
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7444111148322369536

Your STEM OPT can get denied…

even if your company is 100% legit.

Meet Hamsa.

He filed his STEM perfectly, but still woke up to an RFE.

USCIS said his employer didn’t exist in E-Verify.

But he was literally sitting in their office.

Here’s what actually went wrong.

Hamsa's offer letter and pay stubs had his company's legal entity name.

But the E-Verify account was under the parent company.

To USCIS, that is a complete mismatch.

And to make it worse, he had already switched to a new job.

So how do you fix this without getting denied?

First, do not panic.

Go to your past organization HR and get a letter on official letterhead proving the parent company owns the subsidiary, and include the exact E-Verify ID.

Second, do NOT hide your new job.

Show USCIS your updated I-20 and a new I-983.

Because if this happens again, it’s an instant denial.

Btw, I am not an immigration lawyer, but I host live sessions breaking down exact RFE strategies with an Immigration Lawyer with Manifest Law

Save this video right now so you know exactly what to tell your HR if this happens to you,

Here is the link to our next live session Register Now: https://luma.com/7jpareq7
